- **Notes:** Chantway songs are sacred and sequence-bound; reproduce the *principle* (voice-register alternation, steady pulse), not the songs.
- **Source:** Matthews W. 1902 'The Night Chant, a Navaho Ceremony' (AMNH Memoirs 6); McAllester D.P. 1954 'Enemy Way Music' (Peabody Museum Papers 41:3); Frisbie C. 1967 'Kinaaldá'; Wyman L.C. 1970 'Blessingway'; Smithsonian Folkways FW04401 'Music of the American Indians of the Southwest' (Rhodes)

### Native American Church peyote songs — water drum and gourd rattle

- **Evidence level:** traditional (a lineage's own teaching, not a tested claim)
- **Finding:** Peyote meetings run from sunset to sunrise in a tipi around a crescent altar; each singer sings 4 songs in turn with a fixed sequence (Opening Song, Midnight Water Song, Morning Water Song, Quitting Song); accompaniment is a continuous fast duple pulse on a water drum (iron kettle, water inside, thumb pressure holds pitch which 'drops a fifth or more' when released between songs) plus gourd rattle, while the melody is largely ametric, descending in terraces, with vocables ('he ne yo we'). McAllester's 1949 monograph is the foundational analysis.
- **Usable parameters:** Water-drum pulse: fast duple, measured recordings typically ~2.5-3.3 Hz (150-200 bpm) — this figure is from listening, NOT from a published tempo table; drum pitch ~80-120 Hz with glissando 'drop of a fifth' between songs; rattle doubles the pulse; 4-song units.
- **Notes:** No published bpm figure located; McAllester's transcriptions should be checked if a precise number is needed. Peyote use is sacramental and legally restricted; the songs belong to the NAC.
- **Source:** McAllester D.P. 1949 'Peyote Music', Viking Fund Publications in Anthropology 13; Nettl B. 1953 'Observations on meaningless peyote song texts', JAF 66; Canyon Records CR-6054 etc. (Primeaux & Mike); Britannica 'Peyote music'; Stewart O. 1987 'Peyote Religion: A History'

### Inuit katajjaq (throat games) and qilaut drum dance

- **Evidence level:** **strong**
- **Finding:** Katajjaq: two women face to face, each repeating a short motif (2/4 or 6/8, fast) offset by half a bar, alternating voiced/unvoiced and inhaled/exhaled sounds so that one's gap is filled by the other's sound; the pair speeds up and changes motif until one laughs or runs out of breath (games last ~1-3 min). Nattiez's analyses define the unit by morpheme, rhythm, contour, voicing pattern and breath pattern. The Inuit drum dance uses the qilaut (large single-headed frame drum struck on the rim) at a slow pulse with personal pisiit songs; angakkuq (shaman) séances historically used the drum in a dark snowhouse.
- **Usable parameters:** Two-voice interlock: motif length 1 bar, offset 0.5 bar, tempo 120-160 bpm accelerating ~10% per 20 s; voiced/unvoiced inhale-exhale alternation = a 4-8 Hz amplitude pulsation on a breathy drone; qilaut pulse ~1.5-2 Hz, drum ~60-90 Hz with strong rim transient.
- **Notes:** Katajjaq is a game, not a trance technique, but its interlocked breath-pulsation is one of the few vocal traditions that produces a literal 4-8 Hz amplitude modulation.
- **Source:** Nattiez J.-J. 1983 'Some Aspects of Inuit Vocal Games', Ethnomusicology 27(3):457-475; Nattiez 1999 'Inuit throat-games and Siberian throat singing: a comparative, historical and semiological approach', Ethnomusicology 43(3):399-418; Smithsonian Folkways FW04444 'Eskimo Songs from Alaska'; Rasmussen K. 1929 'Intellectual Culture of the Iglulik Eskimos'

### Mesoamerica: Aztec death whistle, huehuetl/teponaztli, Maya trumpets, Chichén Itzá 'quetzal' echo

- **Evidence level:** moderate
- **Finding:** Frühholz et al. 2024 (n=70 raters, 2,500+ sounds; fMRI) showed Aztec skull whistles produce a rough, high-pitched noisy scream-like sound via turbulent air collision in two chambers, perceived as aversive/fear-related and most similar to primate screams and shrill machine noise — i.e., a 'startle' not a 'trance' instrument (burial context at Tlatelolco with Ehecatl-Quetzalcoatl). At Chichén Itzá's Kukulkan pyramid a hand clap at the base of the staircase returns a descending chirped echo matching a quetzal call (Lubman 1998; Declercq et al. 2004 model via periodic step diffraction); ten synchronized clappers raise echo ~10 dB. Aztec huehuetl (upright footed drum) and teponaztli (two-key slit drum) accompanied ritual dance; Maya long wooden trumpets appear in Bonampak murals.
- **Usable parameters:** Chirped echo: descending glide ~1.5 kHz -> ~0.8 kHz over ~150 ms (repetition-pitch effect) — usable as an environmental 'answer' motif; teponaztli two pitches a 3rd-5th apart; huehuetl ~70-120 Hz. Death whistle: avoid in a calming track.
- **Notes:** The Chichén echo may be partly a product of restoration; the 'intended design' interpretation is contested.
- **Source:** Frühholz S. et al. 2024 'Human skull whistles...', Communications Psychology 2:108, doi:10.1038/s44271-024-00157-7 (PMC11555264); Lubman D. 1998 'Archaeological acoustic study of chirped echo from the Mayan pyramid at Chichén Itzá', JASA 104:1763; Declercq et al. 2004 JASA 116(6):3328-3335; Both A.A. 2002/2005 on Mesoamerican organology; Stevenson R. 1968 'Music in Aztec and Inca Territory'

### Chavín de Huántar (Peru, 1200-500 BCE): pututu conch trumpets and gallery acoustics

- **Evidence level:** **strong**
- **Finding:** 20 playable Strombus galeatus pututus were excavated in 2001; CCRMA measurements of 19 give fundamental sounding frequencies of 272-340 Hz (most 290-340 Hz, i.e., D4-F4 region) with a 2nd sounding mode at ~550-700 Hz, up to 111 dBA at 1 m. Because the fundamentals cluster within a few Hz of each other, several played together produce strong acoustic beats and 'phantom' tones (Kolar 2014), and the narrow stone galleries (e.g., Lanzón, Laberintos) are reverberant and disorienting; Chavín iconography (San Pedro cactus, transforming figures) suggests altered-state ritual.
- **Usable parameters:** Two to four conch-like tones at e.g. 300.9, 304.9, 306.2, 311.6 Hz (actual Chavín values) -> beats of 1-11 Hz, with 2nd modes ~602-629 Hz; long reverberation (RT 2-4 s) and strong low-mid resonance; use as the 'upper-world/ascent' call.
- **Notes:** The 'altered-state' function is inferred from iconography and spatial design, not demonstrated.
- **Source:** Cook P.R., Abel J., Kolar M., Huang P., Huopaniemi J., Rick J., Chafe C., Chowning J. 2010 'Acoustic Analysis of the Chavín Pututus', ASA Cancun (ccrma.stanford.edu/groups/chavin/publications/ASA2010_ChavinPututus.pdf); Kolar M. 2013 PhD diss. Stanford 'Archaeological Psychoacoustics at Chavín de Huántar'; Kolar 2014 ASA 'Pututus, resonance and beats'; Kolar 2018 Acoustics Today 14(4) 'Archaeoacoustics: Re-Sounding Material Culture'; Rick J. 2008

### Pre-Columbian Peruvian whistling vessels (Vicús, Moche, Chimú, Inca)

- **Evidence level:** moderate
- **Finding:** Garrett & Statnekov analysed 73 whistling bottles from 9 cultures (JASA 1977): fundamentals cluster by culture — ~1320 Hz (Vicús/Moche/Huari), ~2000 Hz (Recuay), ~2670 Hz (Chancay/Chimú/Inca). When several vessels are blown together their close high pitches generate audible difference tones/beats; one amateur reanalysis of 31 Chimú bottles found pairwise beat frequencies of 5-114 Hz (median ~39 Hz). Statnekov's claim that group playing induces altered states is anecdotal.
- **Usable parameters:** Cluster of 3-6 breathy whistle tones 1300-2700 Hz detuned by 5-40 Hz -> inter-tone beating and low difference tones; short breath-length phrases (2-4 s) with group overlap.
- **Notes:** Only the acoustics are peer-reviewed; the consciousness claims are not.
- **Source:** Garrett S. & Statnekov D.K. 1977 'Peruvian whistling bottles', JASA 62(2):449-453; Statnekov D.K. 1987 'Animated Earth' (North Atlantic Books); NYT coverage (peruvianwhistles.com/nytimes.html)

### Andes: Q'ero paqos, Quechua/Aymara siku and quena, three pachas

- **Evidence level:** traditional (a lineage's own teaching, not a tested claim)
- **Finding:** Aymara/Quechua siku (panpipe) tradition splits a scale across two half-instruments (ira 'leader' and arka 'follower') so a melody is hocketed between two players; troops (tropa) play in parallel octaves/fifths with large drums (bombo/wankara) in processions lasting hours. Q'ero (Cusco) paqos (altomisayoq/pampamisayoq) work with hummed/whispered prayers, coca, the pututu conch and despacho offerings within a three-level cosmos: Hanan Pacha (upper), Kay Pacha (this world), Ukhu Pacha (inner/lower).
- **Usable parameters:** Hocket: alternate notes between L and R channels at 3-5 notes/sec; parallel 5ths/octaves on breathy panpipe timbre (strong odd harmonics); quena ~440-1800 Hz; bombo 60-90 Hz at 1.5-2.5 Hz pulse.
- **Notes:** 'Q'ero mystical tradition' as marketed internationally (Munay-Ki etc.) is a modern synthesis; the three-pacha scheme is authentic Andean.
- **Source:** Turino T. 1993 'Moving Away from Silence' (Univ. Chicago Press); Baumann M.P. 1996 'Cosmología y Música en los Andes'; Wilcox J. 1999 'Keepers of the Ancient Knowledge' (Q'ero); Smithsonian Folkways 'Mountain Music of Peru' (SFW40020/40406); Stobart H. 2006 'Music and the Poetics of Production in the Bolivian Andes'

### Shipibo-Conibo (Ucayali): icaros and kené designs as 'visual music'

- **Evidence level:** traditional (a lineage's own teaching, not a tested claim)
- **Finding:** Shipibo onanya (healers) sing icaros (bewá / masha / shiro bewá) during ayahuasca (oni) ceremonies; traditional explanation holds that each kené geometric design corresponds to a song and that a design can be 'sung onto' a patient's body/aura (Gebhart-Sayer's 'aesthetic therapy'). Documented ceremony sequence: a general icaro for all present, 3-4 more general songs, then individual patients called forward for specific icaros; singing is often preceded or interleaved with whistling (silbando) and accompanied by a shacapa leaf-bundle rattle. Brabec de Mori argues the kené-ayahuasca-song link is a late-20th-c. elaboration.
- **Usable parameters:** Icaro features: high tessitura (male falsetto/female head voice), narrow range, repetitive descending phrases of ~4-8 s, pulse ~90-120 bpm, shacapa rattle shaking at ~2-3 shakes/sec, whistled introduction 20-60 s; ceremony 4-6 h at night, songs 5-20 min each.
- **Notes:** Icaros are considered the property of the singer/plant; synthesize the *form*, don't imitate recordings.
- **Source:** Gebhart-Sayer A. 1985 'The Geometric Designs of the Shipibo-Conibo in Ritual Context', Journal of Latin American Lore 11(2):143-175; Gebhart-Sayer 1987 'Die Spitze des Bewusstseins'; Illius B. 1987 'Ani Shinan'; Brabec de Mori B. 2015 'Sonic Substances and Silent Sounds' (Tipití 13:2); Brabec de Mori & Mori Silvano de Brabec 2009 'Shipibo-Konibo Art and Healing Concepts'; Tournon J. 2002

### Upper Amazon mestizo vegetalismo (Beyer, Luna, Narby) — ceremony sound structure

- **Evidence level:** traditional (a lineage's own teaching, not a tested claim)
- **Finding:** Mestizo ayahuasqueros learn icaros from plant 'teachers' during months-long dieta (isolation, bland food, abstinence). A night ceremony (~4-6 h, starting ~21:00) proceeds: silence/mapacho tobacco smoke -> whistled icaros as the brew takes effect (~30-45 min) -> sung icaros with shacapa -> individual 'soplada' blowing and sucking cures -> closing songs near dawn. Narby's 'The Cosmic Serpent' documents shamans' claims that knowledge arrives as song and image together.
- **Usable parameters:** Phase template: 30-45 min quiet/whistle -> 2-3 h sung icaros (5-15 min each) -> 30 min closing; rattle 2-3 Hz; whistle 800-2000 Hz; no drum.
- **Notes:** The pharmacology (DMT/β-carbolines) is inseparable from the music's reported effects; sound alone is not 'ayahuasca without ayahuasca'.
- **Source:** Beyer S.V. 2009 'Singing to the Plants: A Guide to Mestizo Shamanism in the Upper Amazon' (Univ. New Mexico Press); Luna L.E. 1986 'Vegetalismo: Shamanism among the Mestizo Population of the Peruvian Amazon'; Luna & Amaringo 1991 'Ayahuasca Visions'; Narby J. 1998 'The Cosmic Serpent'; Dobkin de Rios 1972 'Visionary Vine'

### Santo Daime (Brazil) hinários — rhythm types, tempi and session length

- **Evidence level:** moderate
- **Finding:** Daime 'works' are of two kinds: concentração (seated silent meditation with hymns) and hinário/bailado (standing, dancing in formation and singing full hymn-books while shaking maracás in unison). Hymn rhythms are marcha (4/4, ~100-150 bpm), valsa (3/4, ~90-120 bpm) and mazurca (6/8 or 6/4, ~90-120 bpm, rarest). Hinário works typically last 6-12 hours overnight; Mestre Irineu's 'O Cruzeiro' contains 129 hymns.
- **Usable parameters:** Maracá: unison shaker at 100-150 bpm (1.7-2.5 Hz) with strong downbeat; march/waltz alternation to change bodily rhythm; multi-hour unbroken song cycles.
- **Notes:** Documented tempo ranges are from practitioner sources (nextOHM), not from a metronomic study.
- **Source:** Labate B.C. & Pacheco G. 2010 'Opening the Portals of Heaven: Brazilian Ayahuasca Music' (LIT Verlag); Rehen L. 2007 'Texto e contexto da música no Santo Daime' (ResearchGate 311865105); Chacruna.net 'Music and Body in the Brazilian Santo Daime Tradition'; santodaime.nextohm.com/Instruments/Maraca

### Shuar, Yanomami, Huni Kuin (Kaxinawá) — other Amazonian sound practices

- **Evidence level:** traditional (a lineage's own teaching, not a tested claim)
- **Finding:** Shuar/Achuar: uwishin (shamans) sing anent (whispered/sung spirit songs) and suck/blow tsentsak 'darts' under natem (ayahuasca) and tobacco juice; Harner's 1961 fieldwork founded his later core shamanism. Yanomami: shapori inhale yãkoana (Virola) snuff daily and chant/dance for hekura spirits, sessions of 1-3 h with stomping, shouting and sung dialogue. Huni Kuin: huni meka songs (pakarin, kayatibu, dautibu) sung in nixi pae (ayahuasca) rituals, with kené patterns also central; women sing in high register, men in call-response.
- **Usable parameters:** Anent: whispered-sung, near-monotone on 2-3 pitches, 60-80 bpm; Yanomami hekura chant: loud, call-response, 1-3 h; Huni Kuin: unison group chant with sudden register leaps.
- **Notes:** Harner's generic 'Lower/Upper world' journey is a composite; Shuar cosmology emphasises waterfalls, arutam visions and specific spirit types rather than a generic three-tier map.
- **Source:** Harner M. 1972 'The Jívaro: People of the Sacred Waterfalls'; Descola P. 1996 'The Spears of Twilight' (anent); Lizot J. 1985 'Tales of the Yanomami'; Chagnon N. 1968 'Yanomamö'; Lagrou E. 2007 'A Fluidez da Forma' (Kaxinawá); Kensinger K. 1995 'How Real People Ought to Live'

### Bwiti (Gabon, Fang/Mitsogo): ngombi harp and mongongo mouth-bow in iboga initiation

- **Evidence level:** traditional (a lineage's own teaching, not a tested claim)
- **Finding:** Bwiti initiation (bwiti/missoko) involves eating large doses of Tabernanthe iboga root bark; initiations last 3-5 days for men and up to weeks for women, with all-night music sessions around a fire. The 8-string ngombi harp is the 'voice' of the female principle Nyingwan Mebege and leads the night; the mongongo (mouth-resonated musical bow struck with a stick) carries the dead/ancestors' voice; drums (obaka), soke rattles, bells and antelope horns complete the ensemble, with the music's polyrhythm and continuity timed to iboga's 12-24 h course (visions, then 'questioning' by elders).
- **Usable parameters:** Ngombi: 8 strings pentatonic ~150-500 Hz, continuous ostinato 2-bar cycles at ~110-130 bpm, no gaps for hours; mongongo: single bow fundamental ~100-150 Hz with mouth-swept harmonics; night-long session shape (dusk->dawn).
- **Notes:** No measured tempi located; the sound is bound to iboga pharmacology and to a Christian-syncretic (Fang Bwiti) liturgy.
- **Source:** Fernandez J.W. 1982 'Bwiti: An Ethnography of the Religious Imagination in Africa' (Princeton UP); Fernandez 1972 'Tabernanthe iboga: narcotic ecstasis and the work of the ancestors' in Furst (ed.) 'Flesh of the Gods'; Swiderski S. 1970-71 'Studies on the iboga cults' series; Ocora C560077 'Gabon: Musique des Mitsogho et des Batéké'; This Moment Records 'Calling the Spirits: Missoko Bwiti Music of Gabon'

### Ju|'hoansi (!Kung) San healing dance — n|om and !kia

- **Evidence level:** moderate
- **Finding:** The Kalahari healing dance is held at night, often all night (4-12+ h): women sit in a circle around the fire singing n|om songs (giraffe, eland, sun) and clapping interlocking polyrhythms (typically 3-against-2 / 12-pulse cycles); men dance a shuffling circle behind them with leg rattles; after 1-2 h, healers' n|om 'boils' in the belly and rises up the spine into !kia (trance), during which they lay hands on everyone, pull out sickness, and may travel to God's village; experienced healers are talked/rubbed down from !kia by others (a human 'callback').
- **Usable parameters:** Clapping: 12-pulse cycle at ~100-130 bpm (pulse 1.7-2.2 Hz) with 3:2 cross-rhythm; women's polyphonic yodel-like singing in overlapping entries, range ~200-800 Hz; leg-rattle shuffle at 2 steps/sec; build-up 60-120 min before trance; no drum.
- **Notes:** No single measured tempo exists; the ethnography stresses hours of sustained rhythm + heat + community, not a specific frequency.
- **Source:** Katz R. 1982 'Boiling Energy: Community Healing among the Kalahari Kung' (Harvard UP); Katz, Biesele & St Denis 1997 'Healing Makes Our Hearts Happy'; Marshall L. 1969 'The medicine dance of the !Kung Bushmen', Africa 39; Lewis-Williams J.D. 1981 'Believing and Seeing' (rock-art link); British Library Sound Archive C1101 Ju|'hoansi healing songs (recorded 2006-)

### Gnawa lila (Morocco) — guembri, qraqeb and the seven colour suites

- **Evidence level:** traditional (a lineage's own teaching, not a tested claim)
- **Finding:** The lila is an overnight (8+ h) possession-healing ceremony led by a maalem on the guembri (3-string bass lute, ~55-110 Hz strings, thumb-struck) with a chorus playing qraqeb iron castanets in interlocking patterns and singing responses; the night moves through a fixed order of seven colour-coded suites (mluk: white, black/'ghaba', blue, red, green, yellow, multicoloured/Aisha), each with its own rhythms, incense and spirits; within each piece the tempo accelerates gradually (ternary 6/8 sliding toward binary feel) until dancers fall into jedba trance. Fuson and Kapchan are the main ethnomusicological sources.
- **Usable parameters:** Guembri ostinato 55-110 Hz; qraqeb at 8th-note density ~4-6 strikes/sec on a 6/8 cycle; piece-level accelerando from ~100 to ~160+ bpm over 5-15 min; 7-suite macro-structure (usable as 7 colour-coded sections).
- **Notes:** BPM values are observational; no metronomic study was found. Stock loops labelled 'Gnawa 140 bpm' are not ethnographic data.
- **Source:** Fuson T.D. 2009 PhD diss. UC Berkeley 'Musicking Tradition in Place: Participation, Values, and Banks in Bamako / Gnawa' (see also Fuson 2001 on lila structure); Kapchan D. 2007 'Traveling Spirit Masters: Moroccan Gnawa Trance and Music in the Global Marketplace' (Wesleyan UP); Pâques V. 1991 'La religion des esclaves'; Axiom/Island 'The Trance of Seven Colors' (Maleem Mahmoud Ghania & Pharoah Sanders 1994); Hell B. 2002 'Le tourbillon des génies'

### Zar (Sudan/Egypt/Ethiopia) and Hausa bori — possession-cult music

- **Evidence level:** traditional (a lineage's own teaching, not a tested claim)
- **Finding:** Zar: 4-9 h women's ceremonies; each spirit has its own 'thread' (khayt) song; songs run 5-20 min, with drums (dallūka, tabla, mazhar frame drums) and the Sudanese tanbura bowl-lyre accelerating until the spirit 'descends'; nearly 50% of ever-married women in Boddy's Hofriyat study were possessed. Bori (Hausa): spirits (iskoki) 'mount' their 'horses' (dancers) while the goge one-string fiddle and kwarya calabash drums play each spirit's signature tune (kirari praise-epithets), with specialised musicians (maroka).
- **Usable parameters:** Per-spirit signature motif (2-4 bars) repeated with accelerando ~90 -> 150 bpm over 5-20 min; frame-drum density doubles near climax; tanbura: 5-6 strings ~110-330 Hz; goge: single string ~200-400 Hz with wide vibrato.
- **Notes:** Possession (spirit comes into the person) is the opposite movement from soul-flight (person goes out); the user's goals mix both — the design should pick one metaphor per section.
- **Source:** Boddy J. 1989 'Wombs and Alien Spirits: Women, Men and the Zar Cult in Northern Sudan' (Univ. Wisconsin Press); Boddy 1988 Am. Ethnologist 15(1); Kenyon S. 2012 'Spirits and Slaves in Central Sudan'; Besmer F.E. 1983 'Horses, Musicians and Gods: The Hausa Cult of Possession-Trance' (Bergin & Garvey); Erlmann V. 1982 on bori music

### Yoruba bàtá/Ifá, Cuban Santería batá toques, Brazilian Candomblé atabaques

- **Evidence level:** traditional (a lineage's own teaching, not a tested claim)
- **Finding:** Yoruba bàtá (iyá ìlù, omele abo, omele ako/kudi) is a speech-surrogate drum of Ṣàngó's cult encoding tone-language (Villepastour); Ifá divination verses (ese Ifá) are chanted, not drummed. Cuban Lucumí batá (iyá, itótele, okónkolo) play the oru seco/oru del igbodu: a fixed sequence of ~22-24 toques for the orishas, exploring every 4/4-6/8 permutation, with the okónkolo as timekeeper, before the sung oru cantado; possession ('montar') is triggered by the iyá's conversational calls and specific toques (e.g., chachalokefun for Changó). Candomblé: rum, rumpi and lê atabaques plus agogô play toques (alujá, ijexá, bravum, etc.) in the xirê, a fixed order from Exu to Oxalá, during which filhos-de-santo are 'taken' by orixás.
- **Usable parameters:** Batá: okónkolo pulse ~2-3 Hz, iyá low-head 'enú' ~70-100 Hz, toque sections 2-5 min, 6/8 ↔ 4/4 ambiguity (hemiola) as the trance device; Candomblé: rum improvises over 8-12 bar cycles at 100-180 bpm; ordered 'procession' of spirit sections.
- **Notes:** No published bpm tables located; tempi are from performance practice.
- **Source:** Villepastour A. 2010 'Ancient Text Messages of the Yorùbá Bàtá Drum: Cracking the Code' (Ashgate/SOAS); Amira J. & Cornelius S. 1992 'The Music of Santería: Traditional Rhythms of the Batá Drums' (White Cliffs Media); Mason J. 1992 'Orin Orisa'; Béhague G. 1984 on Candomblé; Smithsonian Folkways SFW40419 'Sacred Rhythms of Cuban Santería'

### Haitian Vodou: Rada vs Petwo drumming and the kase (break)

- **Evidence level:** traditional (a lineage's own teaching, not a tested claim)
- **Finding:** Rada rites (Dahomean lwa: Legba, Damballa) use three drums (manman, segon, boula) with yanvalou (6/8-12/8, slow-medium, undulating 'serpent' body movement), mahi and zepòl; Petwo rites (Kongo/'hot' lwa) use two drums played with hands, faster and tenser (petwo, kita, boumba). The master drummer's kase ('break') — a sudden cross-rhythmic figure against the ongoing pattern — destabilizes dancers and precipitates possession ('the lwa mounts the horse'); the ogan iron bell keeps the timeline.
- **Usable parameters:** Yanvalou: 12/8 at ~90-110 bpm (pulse 1.5-1.8 Hz), ogan timeline 7-stroke; Petwo: 4/4 at ~140-180 bpm; kase: 2-4 bar cross-rhythmic interruption every 1-3 min as a 'tipping' device; drums 80-250 Hz.
- **Notes:** BPM figures are descriptive, not metronomically published.
- **Source:** Wilcken L. 1992 'The Drums of Vodou' (White Cliffs Media); Métraux A. 1959 'Voodoo in Haiti'; Deren M. 1953 'Divine Horsemen' (and 1985 film); Courlander H. 1960 'The Drum and the Hoe'; Smithsonian Folkways FW04432 'Drums of Haiti'; Averill G. 1997

### Ethiopian Orthodox zema (St Yared, 6th c.)

- **Evidence level:** traditional (a lineage's own teaching, not a tested claim)
- **Finding:** Yared's chant has three modes: Ge'ez (plain, ordinary days, 'Father'), Ezel (measured/gentle, fasts and funerals, 'Son'), Araray (ornamented, light-beat, festivals, 'Holy Spirit'), notated in the Deggua with melekket signs; liturgical performance (aquaquam) uses the kebero double-headed drum, tsenatsil sistrum, prayer staffs (meqwamiya) and swaying dance, building over hours in vigils (mahlet) that run through the night.
- **Usable parameters:** Three modal 'colours' mapped to three sections; kebero: low head ~60-80 Hz, high ~150 Hz, slow pulse 1-1.5 Hz accelerating in aquaquam; sistrum shake on every 2nd beat.
- **Notes:** No tempo tables; descriptions of mode mood vary across sources.
- **Source:** Shelemay K.K. & Jeffery P. 1993-97 'Ethiopian Christian Liturgical Chant: An Anthology' (3 vols, A-R Editions); Shelemay, Jeffery & Monson 1993 'Oral and written transmission in Ethiopian Christian chant', Early Music History 12; arXiv 2412.18784 'Zema Dataset'; Wikipedia 'Orthodox Tewahedo music'

### Aka / Baka (BaYaka) polyphony and spirit plays

- **Evidence level:** traditional (a lineage's own teaching, not a tested claim)
- **Finding:** Aka polyphony (UNESCO 2003/2008) layers four independent voice parts with hocket, yodel (chest/head alternation) and echo repetition over cyclic 8-12 pulse patterns; Arom's re-recording method revealed the underlying cycles. Baka women's yélī yodelled polyphony is a ritual act protecting men on spear hunts; BaYaka mokondi massana spirit plays use women's clapped polyrhythms and hocketed yodel to draw forest spirits (bobe, ejengi) into camp at night.
- **Usable parameters:** 4-voice cyclic hocket on a 12-pulse cycle at ~120-140 bpm; yodel leaps of a 5th-octave; dense interlock yields ~4-8 note-onsets/sec (a de facto theta-rate onset stream).
- **Notes:** No 'trance' is claimed for Aka/Baka; the function is spirit-attraction and social synchrony.
- **Source:** Arom S. 1991 'African Polyphony and Polyrhythm' (Cambridge UP); Fürniss S. 2006/2021 'Diyei and yeli: yodeling in two musical cultures of Central Africa'; Lewis J. 2013 'A cross-cultural perspective on the significance of music and dance... BaYaka'; Ocora C559012 'Centrafrique: Anthologie de la musique des Pygmées Aka' (UNESCO Collection, Smithsonian Folkways)

### Yolngu yidaki (didgeridoo), bilma clapsticks, manikay song cycles and the bullroarer

- **Evidence level:** **strong**
- **Finding:** The didjeridu/yidaki is a lip-reed drone of 55-80 Hz (three measured Arnhem Land instruments: 60, 64, 80 Hz; players favour B1-E2, 61.7-82.4 Hz, with C#2 ≈ 69.3 Hz preferred); timbre is shaped by vocal-tract resonances in the 1-2 kHz band and by circular breathing; in Yolngu manikay (clan song series) the yidaki supports a songman with bilma (hardwood clapsticks) whose patterns and the song texts map ancestral tracks ('songlines'/Dreaming). Bullroarers (restricted men's objects in many groups) produce 40-160 Hz peak energy at arm-swing rates of 1-2 Hz with no appreciable infrasound (<20 Hz).
- **Usable parameters:** Drone 61.7-82.4 Hz (C#2 = 69.3 Hz) with 'toot' overtone ~10th above (~190-250 Hz), formant sweeps 1-2 kHz at 0.5-2 Hz; rhythmic tonguing/diaphragm pulses at 2-6 Hz; bilma 2-4 Hz with strong 3-5 kHz transient; bullroarer whirr 70-120 Hz with 1-2 Hz AM.
- **Notes:** In Yolngu law the yidaki is a men's instrument in ceremony and some bullroarer sounds are secret-sacred; use synthesized timbres, not ceremonial recordings. Didgeridoo playing has one RCT benefit (sleep apnoea: Puhan et al. 2006 BMJ).
- **Source:** Tarnopolsky, Fletcher, Hollenberg, Lange, Smith & Wolfe 2005 'The vocal tract and the sound of a didgeridoo', Nature 436:39; Fletcher N.H. 1996 'The didjeridu (didgeridoo)', Acoustics Australia 24:11-15; UNSW Music Acoustics 'Didjeridu acoustics' (phys.unsw.edu.au/jw/dij/dij.html); Fletcher N.H. 2002 'Australian Aboriginal musical instruments — the bullroarer', AAS 2002 proceedings; Neuenfeldt K. (ed.) 1997 'The Didjeridu: From Arnhem Land to Internet'; Magowan F. 2007 'Melodies of Mourning' (Yolngu); Marett A. 2005 'Songs, Dreamings and Ghosts'

### Māori taonga pūoro and karakia

- **Evidence level:** traditional (a lineage's own teaching, not a tested claim)
- **Finding:** Taonga pūoro (revived by Hirini Melbourne, Richard Nunns, Brian Flintoff) include the pūtōrino (20-50 cm bugle-flute with three 'voices': male trumpet, female flute, and the 'wheke' crying voice of Hine-raukatauri, goddess of flute music), kōauau (3-hole bone/wood flute), pūtātara (conch trumpet), pūrerehua (bullroarer, 'brings rain') and pūmotomoto (played over the fontanelle of infants). Karakia are incantations recited rapidly on a monotone without pause for breath (a stumble invalidates them), used to open the way to atua and tūpuna.
- **Usable parameters:** Pūtōrino flute voice ~400-900 Hz breathy with microtonal bends; trumpet voice ~200-300 Hz; kōauau ~600-1200 Hz 3-4 notes within a 4th; karakia: rapid monotone speech-chant ~6-8 syllables/sec (a theta-rate syllable stream) on one pitch ~110-150 Hz.
- **Notes:** No published Hz measurements located; values are from recordings/instrument size.
- **Source:** Flintoff B., Melbourne H. & Nunns R. 2004 'Taonga Pūoro: Singing Treasures' (Craig Potton); Melbourne & Nunns 1994 CD 'Te Ku Te Whe' (Rattle); Nunns R. with Thomas A. 2014 'Te Ara Puoro: A Journey into the World of Māori Music'; Sheehan, Murray & Smith 2026 'Ihirangaranga', AlterNative (SAGE) doi:10.1177/11771801251406142; Shortland E. 1882 'Maori Religion and Mythology' (karakia)

### Hawaiian oli (chant), ipu heke, and the Huna caveat

- **Evidence level:** traditional (a lineage's own teaching, not a tested claim)
- **Finding:** Oli styles: kepakepa (rapid rhythmic recitation for genealogies), kāwele (heightened declamation), olioli (sustained-pitch melodic chant with 'i'i vibrato at phrase ends and long breath lines), ho'āeae (soft, drawn-out vowels, love chants), ho'ouēuē (wailing/lament); hula pahu and ipu heke (double gourd, struck on ground and slapped) give a pulse to mele hula. 'Huna' is NOT an indigenous Hawaiian system: it was invented by Max Freedom Long (1936 'Recovering the Ancient Magic'; 1948 'The Secret Science Behind Miracles'), who never trained with Hawaiian practitioners; Hawaiian scholars reject it as pseudo-Hawaiian.
- **Usable parameters:** Olioli: single sustained pitch ~110-165 Hz male / 220-330 female, phrases 8-15 s on one breath, terminal 'i'i' vibrato ~5-7 Hz; ipu heke: low thump ~80-100 Hz + slap transient at 1.5-2.5 Hz.
- **Notes:** Do not present 'Huna' three-selves/mana concepts as Hawaiian tradition.
- **Source:** Kumukahi.org 'Oli'; Stillman A.K. 2009 'Oli — five genres'; Tatar E. 1982 'Nineteenth Century Hawaiian Chant' (Bishop Museum); Emerson N.B. 1909 'Unwritten Literature of Hawaii'; Chai M. 2011 'Na Mo'olelo Lomilomi'; Kahaleole Hall L. 2005 'Hawaiian at Heart and Other Fictions' (Contemporary Pacific); Rothstein? no — see Charlot J. 'Huna' critique

### Balinese gamelan ombak (paired detuning) — ritual use of literal acoustic beating; kecak and sanghyang

- **Evidence level:** **strong**
- **Finding:** Balinese gamelan metallophones are built in pairs — pengumbang (lower) and pengisep (higher) — deliberately detuned so their unison beats (ombak, 'wave'): gong kebyar typically 7-8 Hz (manuals give 7-10 Hz), gender wayang ~5 Hz, angklung 8-9 Hz, with ~9 Hz the practical Balinese ceiling; tuners hold the beat rate constant across the whole multi-octave range, which forces stretched/compressed octaves (e.g., 400 + 408 Hz, octave 808 not 816). The shimmering 'alive' sound is required for temple ceremonies (odalan). Kecak ('cak' chorus of 50-100 men in interlocking 3-,4-,5-pulse patterns) grew out of the sanghyang trance-exorcism chorus; Walter Spies and Wayan Limbak staged it for tourists in the 1930s.
- **Usable parameters:** Pair every sustained tone with a copy detuned by +5 to +9 Hz (constant Hz offset, not cents) to produce speaker-friendly MONAURAL beating at theta/alpha rates — the closest traditional analogue to a binaural/monaural beat; kecak: 3 interlocking 'cak' voices at 8th-note density ~8 onsets/sec over ~160-180 bpm.
- **Notes:** Ombak is explicitly aesthetic-ritual ('living' sound), not a documented 'brainwave' technology, but it is the one tradition that engineers a fixed 5-9 Hz beat frequency.
- **Source:** Vitale W. & Sethares W. 2021 'Balinese Gamelan Tuning: The Toth Archives', Analytical Approaches to World Music 9(2); Sethares & Vitale 2020 'Ombak and octave stretching in Balinese gamelan', J. Mathematics and Music 16(1) doi:10.1080/17459737.2020.1812128; Paelinck M. & Vitale W. 'Repair and tuning of Balinese gamelan instruments' (swarasanti.nl); Baumbusch 'Lightbulb Ensemble' (Smithsonian NMAA essay); McPhee C. 1966 'Music in Bali'; Tenzer M. 2000 'Gamelan Gong Kebyar'; Bandem & deBoer 1981 (sanghyang)

### Korean mudang gut — janggu, jing, jangdan cycles and tempo arcs

- **Evidence level:** traditional (a lineage's own teaching, not a tested claim)
- **Finding:** Korean shamanic kut uses an ensemble (janggu hourglass drum, buk, jing gong, kkwaenggwari small gong, piri oboe, taepyeongso) playing jangdan rhythmic cycles (e.g., gutgeori 12/8, deongdeokkungi, sajapuri — a fast, strongly accented 4/4 used at exorcistic climaxes on the East Coast); the invitation song (cheongbae-muga) begins slow and soft and accelerates in tempo and volume; the mudang signals tempo changes by gesture and enters trance (charismatic/kangshin type) while dancing on the beat, then speaks as the spirit (gongsu).
- **Usable parameters:** Gutgeori 12/8 at ~60-70 bpm dotted-quarter; climactic sajapuri 4/4 at 150-200 bpm with accents on beats 1-2; whole-piece accelerando 2-3x over 10-20 min; jing low gong ~90-120 Hz, kkwaenggwari ~2-4 kHz clangs at 6-10 strikes/sec.
- **Notes:** Hereditary (sesŭp) shamans do not go into trance; only charismatic (kangshin) mudang do — the music is the same.
- **Source:** Mills S. 2007 'Healing Rhythms: The World of South Korea's East Coast Hereditary Shamans' (Ashgate; SOAS Musicology); Mills S. 2010 'Music in Korean Shaman Ritual' (Durham e-thesis); Kendall L. 1985 'Shamans, Housewives and Other Restless Spirits'; Howard K. 1989 'Bands, Songs and Shamanistic Rituals'; Hesselink N. (ed.) 2006 'Contemporary Directions: Korean Folk Music'

### Nepali jhankri / Tamang bombo — dhyangro drum

- **Evidence level:** traditional (a lineage's own teaching, not a tested claim)
- **Finding:** Himalayan jhankri (Magar, Kirati/Rai-Limbu, Tamang bombo, Sherpa lhawa, Ladakhi lhaba) drum the dhyangro (double-headed frame drum on a carved phurba-dagger handle, struck with a curved cane) while chanting ritual-language mantras; the all-night chinta ritual begins with slow drumming, the shaman's body begins to tremble (the spirit 'descends' or the shaman's soul travels), then continuous drumming + ankle bells + belt bells accompany divination, sucking cures and spirit dialogue, often ending at dawn; apprentices learn to shake (kamnu) and journey.
- **Usable parameters:** Dhyangro: ~30-40 cm frame, ~100-150 Hz, pulse 2-3 Hz rising to 4-6 Hz as shaking begins; bells on body create 8-12 Hz jingle during tremor; sessions 4-10 h overnight.
- **Notes:** Peters (trained by a Tamang bombo) explicitly compares Tamang trance to Harner's journey; Sidky critiques the 'archaic shaman' framing.
- **Source:** Peters L.G. 1981 'Ecstasy and Healing in Nepal: An Ethnopsychiatric Study of Tamang Shamanism' (Undena); Sidky H. 2008 'Haunted by the Archaic Shaman: Himalayan Jhakris and the Discourse on Shamanism' (Lexington); Maskarinec G. 1995 'The Rulings of the Night'; Desjarlais R. 1992 'Body and Emotion' (Yolmo); Hitchcock & Jones (eds) 1976 'Spirit Possession in the Nepal Himalayas'

### Hmong txiv neeb (ua neeb) — gong, finger bells, rattle and the 'horse' bench

- **Evidence level:** traditional (a lineage's own teaching, not a tested claim)
- **Finding:** A Hmong shaman sits veiled on a bench (rooj neeb, the 'horse'), slips bronze finger rings/bells (tswb neeb) on the left hand, holds the hooped rattle (txiab neeb) and divination horns (kuam), while an assistant strikes the gong (nruas neeb) to alert helper spirits; the shaman's body trembles involuntarily and rocks as if galloping, chanting in a special register as she travels through the spirit world to retrieve a soul; a full healing follows an A-B-A shape with two soul-calling sections, and the thrown bells indicate whether the soul has returned.
- **Usable parameters:** Gong strikes ~1-2 Hz (fundamental ~150-250 Hz with long shimmer); finger-bell jingle 8-12 Hz during tremor; rattle 3-5 Hz; chant monotone ~140-180 Hz at 5-7 syllables/sec; A-B-A ritual form.
- **Notes:** Instrument names are inconsistently labelled across online sources (nruas neeb vs tswb neeb).
- **Source:** Symonds P.V. 2004 'Calling in the Soul: Gender and the Cycle of Life in a Hmong Village' (Univ. Washington Press); Lemoine J. 1986 'Shamanism in the context of Hmong resettlement'; Mottin J. 1982 'Allons faire le tour du ciel et de la terre: le chamanisme des Hmong vu dans les textes'; Carleton College 'Hmong Religiosity and Shamanism in the Twin Cities' (religionsmn.carleton.edu); Fadiman A. 1997 'The Spirit Catches You and You Fall Down'

### Ainu (Hokkaido/Sakhalin): mukkuri, tonkori, upopo and tusu

- **Evidence level:** traditional (a lineage's own teaching, not a tested claim)
- **Finding:** Ainu women play the mukkuri (bamboo jaw-harp pulled by string) and the tonkori (5-string zither); upopo are seated round-songs (canon-like, with a clapped lid rhythm), rimse round dances, and yukar epics are chanted with repni stick beating; 'husse' is an exorcist's conjuring breath stylized into a shouted burden; tusu (female shamanic possession/divination, esp. Sakhalin) used the tonkori and drum. Ethnomusicologists debate Ainu music's circumpolar links.
- **Usable parameters:** Mukkuri: fundamental ~90-130 Hz with mouth-formant melody; upopo: canon at 1-2 bar offset, ~100-120 bpm, clapped on a wooden lid; tonkori: 5 open strings ~110-330 Hz ostinato.
- **Notes:** Thin sourcing on tusu specifics; treat as illustrative.
- **Source:** Tanimoto K. 1965-2000 (Hokkaido Univ. of Education) 'A Study on the Tone-system of Ainu Music'; Chiba N. 2008 'Ainu music' in Garland Encyclopedia (East Asia); Savage, Matsumae et al. 2015 'How circumpolar is Ainu music?' (ResearchGate 282351814); Ohnuki-Tierney E. 1973 'The shamanism of the Ainu of the northwest coast of southern Sakhalin', Ethnology 12(1); King Records 'Ainu Songs' (UNESCO Collection, Smithsonian Folkways UNES08047)

### Finnish-Karelian runo (Kalevala) singing, kantele, and the tietäjä

- **Evidence level:** moderate
- **Finding:** Runo-song uses Kalevala metre (trochaic tetrameter with word-initial stress, 8-syllable lines) sung in narrow pentachordal melodies (first five scale degrees), typically in 5/4 (five-beat) or 2/4 lines, by a soloist alternating with a chorus/partner who repeats each line, in incantatory style for epics and loitsu (spells); the tietäjä ('knower', incantation specialist) used loud, 'raised' (nostaa luonto) chanting and sometimes a kantele (5-string diatonic zither, D-E-F#/F-G-A) to shift into an intense state ('haltioituminen'). Sámi joik and Finnish rune-song are distinct traditions.
- **Usable parameters:** 5-beat lines at ~100-120 bpm, pentachord within a fifth, soloist/chorus line-repetition (call-and-echo), continuous for hours; kantele 5 strings tuned D4-A4 region (~294-440 Hz) with drone-like open-string strumming.
- **Notes:** The '5/4' description was not confirmed in this pass by a primary source though it is standard in Finnish musicology.
- **Source:** Siikala A.-L. 2002 'Mythic Images and Shamanism: A Perspective on Kalevala Poetry' (FF Communications 280); Laitinen H. 2003 'Matkoja musiikkiin 1800-luvun Suomessa'; FMQ 'Rune-singing, the musical vernacular' (fmq.fi); Väisänen A.O. 1949 'Kantele- ja jouhikkosävelmiä'; Kuusi, Bosley & Branch 1977 'Finnish Folk Poetry: Epic'

### Norse seiðr and galdr (Eiríks saga rauða; Price)

- **Evidence level:** traditional (a lineage's own teaching, not a tested claim)
- **Finding:** Eiríks saga rauða ch. 4 describes the völva Þorbjörg lítilvölva seated on a raised seiðhjallr platform in Greenland; she requires women to sing varðlok(k)ur ('ward-songs/spirit-locks') in a ring to draw the spirits (náttúrur) before she can prophesy; Guðríðr sings them 'so beautifully that none thought they had heard it sung with so fair a voice'. Galdr is a chanted/shrieked incantation in the galdralag metre (repeating a line with variation); archaeological 'staffs' (seiðstafr) from Fyrkat, Birka etc. are linked to seiðr practitioners. Price interprets seiðr as a Nordic shamanic complex with Sámi parallels.
- **Usable parameters:** Ring of female voices singing a cyclic strophic chant (varðlokkur) around a central seated figure; galdr: high, loud, repetitive incantation with line-repetition (galdralag) — map to a women's-choir section preceding the 'oracle' section; no drum attested.
- **Notes:** No melody survives; all reconstructions (modern 'seiðr') are speculative.
- **Source:** Eiríks saga rauða (Íslenzk fornrit IV; trans. Kunz in 'The Sagas of Icelanders' 2000); Price N. 2002/2019 'The Viking Way: Magic and Mind in Late Iron Age Scandinavia' (Oxbow); Strömbäck D. 1935 'Sejd'; Tolley C. 2009 'Shamanism in Norse Myth and Magic' (FF Communications 296-297); Heide E. 2006 'Gand, seid og åndevind'

### Balkan and Caucasus drone polyphony (Bulgarian diaphony, Georgian Svan)

- **Evidence level:** traditional (a lineage's own teaching, not a tested claim)
- **Finding:** Bulgarian Shop/Pirin diaphony sets a melody over a sung drone with sustained major-second clashes ('izvika' cry-out voice); Georgian polyphony (UNESCO 2001) is drone-based in all regions, with Svaneti the most archaic — three close-voiced parts in dissonant seconds/sevenths within a narrow (4th) range, growing into round-dances (e.g., 'Lile' sun-hymn, naduri work songs with 'krimanchuli' yodel). Sustained major seconds (e.g., 220 vs 247 Hz) physically produce rough beating at 20-30 Hz, i.e., 'roughness' rather than slow beats; no ethnographic source describes these styles as trance technologies.
- **Usable parameters:** Drone + two voices in sustained seconds: 220/247/262 Hz -> 15-27 Hz roughness; narrow-range (within a 4th) melodic cells; antiphonal round-dance pulse ~100-140 bpm.
- **Notes:** Useful as a 'liminal/rough' timbre, not as entrainment.
- **Source:** Jordania J. 2006 'Who Asked the First Question? The Origins of Human Choral Singing'; Jordania 2007 'Georgian traditional polyphony in comparative studies' (ResearchGate 287233223); Rice T. 1994 'May It Fill Your Soul: Experiencing Bulgarian Music' (Univ. Chicago Press); Wesleyan thesis 'The Mystery of Bulgarian Voices'; Berliner Philharmoniker 'Humming bass and rooster's crow' (Georgia)

### Ancient Greek/Mediterranean: rhombos (bullroarer) in Dionysian/Eleusinian rites; Dodona and Delphi

- **Evidence level:** weak
- **Finding:** The rhombos (bullroarer) and tympanon frame drum were Dionysian cult objects: Aeschylus' Edonians fr. 57 describes 'bull-voiced' mimes roaring from somewhere unseen and the 'image of a drum, like subterranean thunder'; Clement of Alexandria (Protrepticus 2.17) lists the rhombos among the 'toys' of the Dionysian mysteries; Eleusis is associated with the ritual cry/gong (the hierophant struck an 'echeion' gong when invoking Kore — Apollodorus FGrH 244 F110b). At Dodona the oracle spoke through the rustling of Zeus's oak and the ringing of bronze cauldrons/chalkeion (Strabo 7 fr. 3; Callimachus). At Delphi the geological ethylene hypothesis (de Boer, Hale & Chanton 2001) is contested (Foster & Lehoux 2007); no acoustic/infrasound mechanism has evidential support.
- **Usable parameters:** Rhombos whirr 70-120 Hz with 1-2 Hz AM 'from an unseen place' (spatialised offstage); tympanon 100-150 Hz at 2-4 Hz; bronze cauldron/gong strikes with long decay (RT 5-10 s) at ~150-400 Hz; rustling-leaves noise bed.
- **Notes:** Greek sources describe effects (ekstasis, enthousiasmos) literarily; no measurable parameters survive.
- **Source:** Aeschylus fr. 57 (Radt) Edonoi; Clement Protrepticus 2.17-18; Strabo Geography 7, fr. 3 (Dodona); de Boer J.Z., Hale J.R. & Chanton J. 2001 'New evidence for the geological origins of the ancient Delphic oracle', Geology 29(8):707-710; Spiller, Hale & de Boer 2002 Clinical Toxicology 40(2); Foster J. & Lehoux D. 2007 'The Delphic Oracle and the ethylene-intoxication hypothesis', Clinical Toxicology 45; Ustinova Y. 2009 'Caves and the Ancient Greek Mind'

### Neolithic chamber resonance: the '110 Hz' archaeoacoustics finding (Hypogeum, Newgrange, Maeshowe, Cividale, Göbekli Tepe)

- **Evidence level:** weak
- **Finding:** Jahn, Devereux & Ibison 1996 measured six Neolithic chambered sites in Britain/Ireland (Wayland's Smithy, Chun Quoit, Cairn Euny, Camster Round, Maeshowe, Newgrange) and found a principal standing-wave resonance between 95 and 120 Hz (mostly 110-112 Hz) — the range of the adult male chanting voice. Cook, Pajot & Leuchter 2008 exposed 30 volunteers to tones at 90/110/120/130 Hz and reported a 110 Hz-specific shift: relative deactivation of left-hemisphere language regions and a swing toward right prefrontal/temporal activity (EEG, not fMRI). Debertolis & Bisconti (SB Research Group) report the Ħal Saflieni Hypogeum 'Oracle Room' resonating at ~70 Hz and ~114 Hz (a male voice at those pitches excites the whole site; a 109 Hz friction drum only partly), the Cividale (Italy) hypogeum at 94 Hz and 103 Hz, and infrasonic peaks (14-27 Hz, ~22 Hz near pillar 18) at Göbekli Tepe Enclosure D; a 2020 arXiv simulation reproduces the Hypogeum's peaks from its geometry (strongest ~114 Hz, not exactly 110). Great Pyramid King's Chamber figures (117-121 Hz) are unpublished/anecdotal; one instrumented recording gave peak amplitude at 49.5 Hz.
- **Usable parameters:** Male-register drone at 110-114 Hz (A2 = 110 Hz) with a secondary ~70 Hz component and a long, dark reverb (RT 3-6 s) emulating a stone chamber; optional 94/103 Hz alternates (Cividale); keep 'infrasound' out (not reproducible on headphones and not evidenced).
- **Notes:** Cook et al. 2008 is small (n=30), single-site, never independently replicated; the 110 Hz 'brain switch' story is heavily amplified by blogs. The Hypogeum's strongest measured peak is ~114 Hz, not 110.
- **Source:** Jahn R.G., Devereux P. & Ibison M. 1996 'Acoustical resonances of assorted ancient structures', JASA 99(2):649-658; Devereux P. 2001 'Stone Age Soundtracks'; Cook I.A., Pajot S.K. & Leuchter A.F. 2008 'Ancient architectural acoustic resonance patterns and regional brain activity', Time and Mind 1(1):95-104 doi:10.2752/175169608783489099; Debertolis P., Coimbra F. & Eneix L. 2015 'Archaeoacoustic analysis of the Ħal Saflieni Hypogeum in Malta', J. Anthropology & Archaeology 3(1):59-79; Debertolis & Bisconti 2013 'Archaeoacoustics analysis and ceremonial customs in an ancient hypogeum' (Cividale); Debertolis, Gullà & Richeldi 2017 'Archaeoacoustic analysis in Enclosure D at Göbekli Tepe' (HASSACC); arXiv:2010.13697 (Hypogeum spectrum simulation); Till R. 2017 'An archaeoacoustic study of the Ħal Saflieni Hypogeum'

### Stonehenge acoustics

- **Evidence level:** moderate
- **Finding:** Fazenda's circular-space modelling predicts strong modal resonances around 48 Hz; Till's claim of a ~10 Hz 'fundamental' for the stone circle is disputed (open-air site has no enclosed room modes); Cox, Fazenda & Greaney's 1:12 scale model (2020) measured reverberation time ~0.6 s at mid frequencies with ~4 dB amplification of speech/music and strong early reflections, i.e., an intimate 'amplified' space rather than a resonator. Blue stones ring when struck (Devereux & Wozencroft, 'lithophone' hypothesis).
- **Usable parameters:** If used: short reverb (RT 0.6 s), dense early reflections, slight +4 dB presence lift; ringing bluestone 'lithophone' tones ~400-2000 Hz with 2-4 s decay.
- **Notes:** Not a trance resonator; 10 Hz 'Stonehenge frequency' claims are unsupported.
- **Source:** Fazenda B. 2013 'The acoustics of Stonehenge', Acoustics Bulletin 38(1):32-37; Cox T.J., Fazenda B.M. & Greaney S.E. 2020 'Using scale modelling to assess the prehistoric acoustics of Stonehenge', Journal of Archaeological Science 122:105218; Till R. 2010 'Songs of the Stones'; Devereux & Wozencroft 2014 'Stone Age Eyes and Ears' (Preseli lithophones); sarsen.org critique of Till

### Eliade's comparative template: three cosmic zones, axis mundi, drum as 'horse', ecstasy as soul-flight

- **Evidence level:** traditional (a lineage's own teaching, not a tested claim)
- **Finding:** Eliade (1951/1964) synthesised Siberian, Central Asian, American and Oceanian data into the schema used by nearly all modern 'journey' practice: three cosmic zones (sky, earth, underworld) joined by a central axis (World Tree/cosmic mountain/pillar aligned to the Pole Star), passable at a 'hole' by the shaman alone; the drum (made from the World Tree's wood) is the 'horse' or 'boat' of flight; Altaic ascent rites climb 7 or 9 notches of a birch pole (tapty) in stages; initiation involves dismemberment and reassembly; the séance ends with the shaman's return and account. Shirokogoroff and Hamayon critique the ecstasy-centred model, emphasising possession and exchange with spirits.
- **Usable parameters:** Session arc: (1) preparation/intention 3-5 min; (2) descent via a known opening (cave, roots, water) 5 min; (3) lower-world work 10-15 min; (4) return to middle; (5) ascent in 7 or 9 steps (tempo/pitch rising per step) 10-15 min; (6) callback and return account 3-5 min.
- **Notes:** The 'universal shaman' is a mid-20th-c. scholarly construct; real traditions differ widely.
- **Source:** Eliade M. 1964 'Shamanism: Archaic Techniques of Ecstasy' (Princeton/Bollingen 76), esp. chs. 5-6, 8; Hamayon R. 1990 'La chasse à l'âme'; Hultkrantz Å. 1973 'A definition of shamanism', Temenos 9; Hoppál M. 2007 'Shamans and Traditions'; Harner 1980 (adaptation)

### Other North American ceremonial sound (Anishinaabe Midewiwin, Cherokee stomp dance, Apache Gaan, Hopi/Zuni)

- **Evidence level:** traditional (a lineage's own teaching, not a tested claim)
- **Finding:** Anishinaabe Midewiwin degrees (4-8 'degrees') are conferred in a lodge with water-drum (mitigwakik) songs recorded on birch-bark song scrolls (Densmore, Hoffman); Cherokee/Muscogee stomp dance runs all night around a fire with a male leader's call-and-response and women's turtle-shell/can leg rattles (shell shakers) at ~2-3 shakes/sec; Apache Gaan (Mountain Spirit/Crown) dancers appear at the four-night Sunrise ceremony to drums and bullroarers; Hopi kachina songs and Zuni Shalako night-long chants use rattles, turtle-shell knee rattles and a low unison male chorus.
- **Usable parameters:** Water-drum pulse 2-3 Hz with pitch-bend; shell-shaker ostinato 2-3 Hz (3-6 kHz white-noise bursts); call-response phrase pairs 2-4 s; low male unison chorus ~100-150 Hz.
- **Notes:** Midewiwin material is restricted; Densmore's scrolls are published but the songs are society property.
- **Source:** Densmore F. 1910/1913 'Chippewa Music' I-II (BAE Bulletins 45, 53); Hoffman W.J. 1891 'The Midē'wiwin or Grand Medicine Society of the Ojibwa' (BAE 7th Annual Report); Speck F. & Broom L. 1951 'Cherokee Dance and Drama'; Basso K. 1966 'The Gift of Changing Woman' (Apache); Smithsonian Folkways FW04394 'Hopi Katcina Songs'; Tedlock B. 1992 'The Beautiful and the Dangerous' (Zuni)

### Other African traditions: Dagara (Malidoma Somé), Tuareg tende, Zulu/Ndebele sangoma ngoma, Ethiopian & Pygmy already covered

- **Evidence level:** traditional (a lineage's own teaching, not a tested claim)
- **Finding:** Dagara (Burkina Faso/Ghana): Somé describes initiation and funeral rituals where the gyil (14-key pentatonic gourd-resonated xylophone with spider-silk buzzers) plays for days to carry grief and open the gateway to the ancestors; Tuareg (Niger/Mali): women's tende mortar-drum with the asak/asakalabo water-drum in tende-n-goumaten curing ceremonies (spirits 'kel essuf' — people of solitude), with the one-string imzad fiddle; Zulu/Nguni sangoma: ngoma drumming and ukubhula divination, ancestors (amadlozi) 'come out' in trance during thwasa training with call-and-response songs and vigorous dance.
- **Usable parameters:** Gyil: pentatonic ostinato ~130-1000 Hz with buzzing (sympathetic membrane) at 3-5 notes/sec for hours; tende: deep mortar-drum ~60-80 Hz at 2-3 Hz with women's chorus and ululation; ngoma: two-tone drum 90/180 Hz at 2-4 Hz with hand-clap offbeats.
- **Notes:** Somé's writings are first-person and partly literary; treat ethnographic details cautiously.
- **Source:** Somé M.P. 1994 'Of Water and the Spirit' (Penguin); Somé 1998 'The Healing Wisdom of Africa'; Rasmussen S. 1995 'Spirit Possession and Personhood among the Kel Ewey Tuareg' (Cambridge UP); Card C. 1982 'Tuareg music and social identity' (PhD, Indiana); Janzen J. 1992 'Ngoma: Discourses of Healing in Central and Southern Africa' (Univ. California Press); Ngubane H. 1977 'Body and Mind in Zulu Medicine'

### Papua New Guinea, Philippines (babaylan) and Ladakh (lhaba) — brief notes

- **Evidence level:** traditional (a lineage's own teaching, not a tested claim)
- **Finding:** PNG highland and Sepik cultures use kundu hourglass drums, garamut slit-gongs (signalling and spirit voices), bamboo flutes played in pairs as 'spirit voices' in men's house initiations (e.g., Iatmul), and bullroarers kept from women; Kaluli (Feld) model song on bird calls and weeping, with gisalo songs sung to make listeners weep. Philippine babaylan/catalonan shamans historically used gongs (agung), kulintang ensembles and chant (dasal) in possession-healing (pag-anito). Ladakhi lhaba/lhamo oracles enter possession to the sound of damaru hand-drums, bells and recited mantras, with the deity 'descending' after minutes of chanting.
- **Usable parameters:** Paired bamboo flutes in hocket ~500-1500 Hz; garamut slit-gong pulses 60-120 Hz; kundu 100-200 Hz; damaru rattle 6-10 Hz; bell 2-4 kHz.
- **Notes:** Illustrative breadth only; limited measured data.
- **Source:** Feld S. 1982 'Sound and Sentiment: Birds, Weeping, Poetics and Song in Kaluli Expression' (Univ. Pennsylvania Press); Bateson G. 1936 'Naven'; Salazar Z. 1999 on babaylan; Scott W.H. 1994 'Barangay'; Day S. 1989 'Embodying Spirits: Village Oracles and Possession Ritual in Ladakh' (PhD, LSE); Kressing F. 2003 'The increase of shamans in contemporary Ladakh', Asian Folklore Studies 62

### Common cross-cultural session shape (synthesis across all traditions above)

- **Evidence level:** moderate
- **Finding:** Across traditions the documented structure is: (a) a quiet/slow opening of 10-45 min (whistling, slow drum, invocation); (b) a long middle of sustained repetitive pulse (typically 2-5 Hz) or accelerating cycles that peak after 60-120 min of continuous sound; (c) a culminating event (possession, !kia, soul-return) signalled by a tempo break or change of instrument; (d) an explicit return/grounding (callback, being talked down, a meal). Sessions run 2-12 h; nothing shorter than ~2 h is attested outside modern core-shamanism's 15-30 min drum journey.
- **Usable parameters:** For a 45-60 min track: 5 min slow open (1-2 Hz), 10 min build (2-4 Hz accelerando), 20-25 min plateau at 4-5 Hz (with 7-8 Hz ombak drone), 2-3 min 'break' event (tempo jump or instrument change), 30-60 s callback, 5 min re-grounding at 1-1.5 Hz; a 15-min 'daily' version = 2 min open, 10 min 4-5 Hz, 30 s callback, 2 min ground.
- **Notes:** Rouget's conclusion is the single most important caution: music does not mechanically cause trance; it triggers culturally learned states.
- **Source:** Synthesis of: Katz 1982; Kapchan 2007; Boddy 1989; Mills 2007; Peters 1981; Powers 1982; Beyer 2009; Harner 1980; Rouget G. 1985 'Music and Trance: A Theory of the Relations between Music and Possession' (Univ. Chicago Press) — the standard comparative study, which argues that no specific rhythm/frequency is universal and that trance depends on cultural expectation and ritual framing

### Design recommendations from this lens

- Spine: a single low frame-drum sample (fundamental ~90-120 Hz, decay <200 ms) at 4.2-5.0 Hz (252-300 bpm) for the core journey block — this is the only tradition-derived tempo that has instrumented measurements (Harner CD = 254.7 bpm) and some EEG support (Maxfield 1990; Gordon et al. 2026 4-Hz tracking). Keep it unaccented and isochronous.
- Callback: follow FSS practice — a clearly different fast rattle or drum (7-10 Hz) for ~30 s at the end of the journey block, then 1-2 min of slow (1-1.5 Hz) grounding beats. If you adopt the practitioner '4 groups of 7 beats / rapid / 4 groups of 7' pattern, label it as a modern convention, not an FSS or indigenous standard.
- Use Balinese ombak as the acoustic model for a speaker-safe beat: pair every sustained drone with a copy offset by a constant +5 to +9 Hz (e.g., 110 Hz + 117 Hz = 7 Hz beat; 69.3 + 75.3 Hz = 6 Hz), exactly as pengumbang/pengisep pairs are tuned. This yields a monaural beat that survives mono playback and mirrors a real ritual practice.
- Low-world register: a didgeridoo-style drone at 61.7-82.4 Hz (C#2 = 69.3 Hz preferred by Arnhem Land players) with slow formant sweeps in the 1-2 kHz band and circular-breathing continuity (no gaps); add a kargyraa-style sub-octave layer (half the vocal F0) for the 'descent' section.
- Middle-world register: a male-register chamber drone at 110-114 Hz with a ~70 Hz partner and a long dark reverb (RT 3-6 s) to evoke the Hypogeum/Newgrange finding; present it as 'archaeoacoustic resonance' with its weak evidence, not as a brain switch.
- Upper-world/ascent: Chavín-type conch cluster — three or four tones at the measured values ~300.9, 304.9, 306.2, 311.6 Hz with second modes ~600-630 Hz, producing 1-11 Hz inter-tone beats; step the ascent in 7 (Evenki) or 9 (Altaic) tiers, raising pitch/tempo slightly per tier.
- Spectral/overtone layer: a Tuvan-style 150 Hz drone with a narrow (~100 Hz bandwidth) formant 'spotlight' sweeping harmonics 6-12 (900-1800 Hz) slowly; use sparingly as the 'voice of the place', not continuously.
- Build-and-break dynamics borrowed from possession traditions (Gnawa, Zar, Korean gut, Vodou kase): one or two accelerando passages (e.g., 100 -> 160 bpm over 5-10 min on a shaker/qraqeb-like layer) and one brief cross-rhythmic 'kase' interruption before the plateau — these are the documented tipping devices.
- Seven-section colour structure (Gnawa mluk) or four-round structure (Lakota inipi/yuwipi) as the macro-form; both are authentic ways to segment a long ceremony and give listeners a map.
- Human-voice elements should be synthesized or original (vocables, monotone karakia-rate 6-8 syllables/sec chant, Finnish-style pentachordal call-and-echo), never sampled icaros, joiks, yuwipi, Yeibichai, Midewiwin or Yolngu manikay — these are owned ceremonial property.
- Include amplitude-pulsed breath textures at 4-8 Hz (katajjaq-style inhale/exhale alternation) as a soft, organic alternative to pure tone beats during the plateau.
- Reserve bullroarer-like 70-120 Hz whirrs with 1-2 Hz AM for brief 'unseen presence' moments, panned and distant (Dionysian/Aboriginal use), and avoid Aztec death-whistle timbres entirely (aversive by design).
- Deliver two lengths: 45-60 min full arc (5 open / 10 build / 20-25 plateau at 4-5 Hz / 2-3 break / 0.5 callback / 5 ground) and a 15-min daily version replicating FSS's 15-min standard.
- Ship the track with explicit journey instructions (intention, departure point, eyes covered, lying down, journal afterwards): Gingras et al. 2014 found the subjective 'journey' effects appeared only with such instructions.

### Cautions from this lens

- No tradition surveyed, and no study located, supports claims that sound opens the pineal gland, aligns a person with 'the universe', confers manifestation ability, control over 'vibration-energy-frequency', or enables travel between dimensions, realms or times. The guide must state this plainly; the cultural material describes culturally framed trance, healing and divination, not physics-defying effects.
- Entrainment evidence is modest: Maxfield 1990 is n=12; Cook et al. 2008 (110 Hz) is n=30, unreplicated; Gingras et al. 2014 found no physiological advantage for drumming over meditation music; Gordon et al. 2026 found the group-level 4-Hz effect non-significant and only an individual-differences correlation (rho ≈ 0.39). Rouget (1985) concluded after a global survey that no rhythm or frequency reliably induces trance by itself.
- Nearly every indigenous practice cited is inseparable from non-acoustic drivers: psychoactive plants (ayahuasca, iboga, peyote, yãkoana, San Pedro), fasting, sleep deprivation, heat, darkness, days-long duration, community and belief. A 45-60 min audio file reproduces only the sound component.
- Several numbers circulating online are unverified or wrong: the '4 sets of 7 beats' callback is not an FSS standard; '110 Hz' is actually 95-120 Hz across sites and ~114 Hz at the Hypogeum; King's Chamber '117-121 Hz' has no peer-reviewed source (one measurement peaked at 49.5 Hz); 'Stonehenge 10 Hz' is disputed; the 'Harner & Tryon 1996 EEG' study could not be located; Göbekli Tepe infrasound figures come from a non-peer-reviewed group; peyote, Gnawa, Vodou, batá and Korean gut tempi given here are observational ranges, not published metronomic data.
- Cultural and legal restrictions: Sámi drums and joiks, Lakota yuwipi songs, Navajo chantways, Midewiwin songs, Yolngu manikay and bullroarer sounds, Shipibo icaros and Aboriginal/PNG secret-sacred instruments are owned or restricted; do not sample ceremonial recordings or market the track as containing them. 'Huna' is a 1930s invention by Max Freedom Long, not Hawaiian tradition. Peyote and ayahuasca are controlled substances in most jurisdictions.
- Safety: avoid listening while driving or operating machinery; rhythmic sensory stimulation can in rare cases provoke seizures in photosensitive/pattern-sensitive epilepsy (documented mainly for flicker; keep any paired visual flicker out); keep playback below 85 dB SPL (ceremonial pututus reach 111 dBA at 1 m — do not emulate that level); people with psychosis history, PTSD or dissociative conditions should not use trance-induction audio unsupervised; infrasound (<20 Hz) is not reproducible on headphones and should not be claimed.
- Avoid the 'universal shaman' flattening: Eliade's three-world/axis-mundi schema and Harner's Lower/Upper World journey are 20th-century syntheses; real traditions differ (possession vs soul-flight; horizontal river cosmos among some Evenki; four-direction structure among Lakota). Present the track's narrative as an inspired composite, not as 'the' ancestral method.

### Skeptic fact-check of this lens

*Overall reliability:* High for an AI-compiled dossier: the great majority of bibliographic citations, dates, catalogue numbers and measured figures checked out, including the two suspicious 2026 papers (Gordon et al. Sci Rep 16:10204; Aparicio-Terrés 2025 Ann NY Acad Sci) and the FSS '5 beats per second' and Gingras 254.7 bpm figures. Faults found are mostly second-order: one source misattributed to a claim it does not support (Sheehan 2026, which also embeds 528 Hz), one garbled dissertation title (Fuson), a wrong fieldwork year (Harner 1961 vs 1956-57), a slightly overstated Gingras count and Boddy percentage, two swapped Folkways catalogue numbers, and an unfound paper (Harner & Tryon 1996) that actually exists but is a mood/sIgA study. The dossier's evidence-level labels and cautions are appropriately conservative; its main weakness is coverage rather than accuracy — it omits several core ethnomusicological trance studies (Berliner, Friedson, Roseman, Becker, Olsen, Reichel-Dolmatoff, tarantism) and most of the modern EEG literature beyond Neher/Maxfield/Gingras/Gordon (Will & Berg 2007, Oohashi 2002, Huels 2021, Konopacki & Madison 2018, Woodside 1997). Web search budget was exhausted this turn; verification used direct fetches of the publishers' pages, Europe PMC, Crossref and the FSS site, and a few items (Boddy's exact percentage, Fuson's exact title) are flagged for confirmation against the printed sources.

| Original claim | Verdict | Corrected statement | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| FSS's own page states 'approximately 5 beats per second' (=300 bpm); Gingras et al. 2014 measured Harner's CD track 2 at 254.7 bpm (4.2 Hz) for 14 min 58 s. | **accurate** | Confirmed: shamanism.org/drumming says the drum is played 'in a steady rhythm at approximately 5 beats per second' with 15- and 30-min solo recordings ending in a 'fast callback rhythm'; Gingras et al. 2014 report a mean tempo of 254.7 bpm (~4.2 Hz), 14 min 58 s of drumming in a 15 min 32 s sequence (Harner CD track 2, callback replaced by the rattle callback from Harner's Rattle CD). Add the nuance that Harner's 1980 book 'The Way of the Shaman' recommends ~205-220 bpm (~3.4-3.7 Hz), so FSS's own numbers span ~3.4-5 Hz; the single instrumented value (4.2 Hz) is the one to cite. | https://www.shamanism.org/drumming/ ; Gingras, Pohler & Fitch 2014 PLOS ONE 9(7):e102103 (journals.plos.org); Harner 1980 ch. 3 |
| Gingras et al. 2014 (n=39) found heaviness, dreamlike imagery and perceived heart-rate drop ONLY when paired with shamanic instructions (10/10 vs 2/8). | **overstated** | n=39 is correct (shamanic drumming 10, relaxation drumming 8, shamanic meditation music 11, relaxation meditation music 10). The 10/10 vs 2/8 figure applies only to dreamlike experiences (p<.001); heaviness was 9/10 vs 3/8 (p=.044) and decreased heart rate 9/10 vs 2/8 (p=.003). Cortisol fell in all conditions (2.93 to 2.58 ng/ml) with no difference between instruction or music conditions. | Gingras, Pohler & Fitch 2014 PLOS ONE doi:10.1371/journal.pone.0102103 (fetched full text) |
| Gordon, Karvat, Dagan & Landau 2026 (n=40, 64-ch EEG, 10-min blocks at 1.5/4/9.5 bps), Scientific Reports 16:10204, doi:10.1038/s41598-026-37700-x: 4 Hz tracking correlated with altered-experience scores (rho=0.39, p=0.01); at group level 9.5 bps > 1.5 bps and 4 bps not different from either. | **accurate** | Verified as real and correctly reported: 'Neural tracking at theta predicts drumming-induced altered states of consciousness', Sci Rep 16:10204 (26 Mar 2026; PMC13022407). 40 recruited, 39 analysed; three 10-min blocks at 1.5/4/9.5 bps; main effect of rhythm F(2,76)=3.1, p=0.05; 4 bps vs 1.5 bps t=0.42 p=0.67, vs 9.5 bps p=0.081; 9.5>1.5 bps p=0.02; 4 Hz coupling vs AE rho=0.39 p=0.01 (and vs mean AE rho=0.32 p=0.04). Authors could not characterise tracking at 9.5 bps because of endogenous alpha. | https://www.nature.com/articles/s41598-026-37700-x ; Europe PMC record |
| Review: Aparicio-Terrés et al. 2025 Ann NY Acad Sci doi:10.1111/nyas.15403 | **accurate** | Verified: Aparicio-Terrés R, López-Mochales S, Díaz-Andreu M, Escera C (2025) 'The neurobiology of altered states of consciousness induced by drumming and other rhythmic sound patterns', Ann N Y Acad Sci 1550(1):55-70 (PMC12412719). Note its conclusion: rhythmic sound mainly fosters absorption and relaxation, neural findings are heterogeneous across studies, and the thalamocortical low-frequency entrainment account is a hypothesis. It reports mood and salivary IgA changes in experienced journeyers; an earlier version is Harner & Tryon 1992 'Psychoimmunological effects of shamanic drumming' in Hoppál & Pentikäinen (eds) Northern Religions and Shamanism. Anyone citing it for EEG/theta entrainment is misattributing. | Shaman (journal of the International Society for Academic Research on Shamanism) vol. 4, 1996; PubMed/Crossref return no record, consistent with a non-indexed journal |

It contains no acoustic measurements of pūtōrino/kōauau or karakia rates and itself uses the pseudo-historical 'Solfeggio' 528 Hz; it should not be cited as evidence for the Māori parameters given. Rely on Flintoff/Melbourne/Nunns 2004 and Nunns & Thomas 2014 instead. | Crossref metadata and abstract for doi:10.1177/11771801251406142 |

Timothy D. Fuson's UC Berkeley ethnomusicology PhD (2009) is on dancing and drumming in the Moroccan Gnawa lila ('Musicking Moves and Grooves: Dancing and Drumming in Moroccan Gnawa Ritual' is the title as commonly cited); verify the exact wording in the UC Berkeley catalogue before citing. Kapchan 2007 and Pâques 1991 are the safer sources for lila structure. | Crossref bibliographic query returns no such title; UC Berkeley Music Dept. dissertation list (Fuson 2009, Gnawa) |

'No published figure' should read 'not consulted in this pass'. | McAllester D.P. 1949 Viking Fund Publ.
