- **Finding:** Schwarz & Taylor measured a robust 40 Hz ASSR to monaural (acoustic) beats at both low and high carriers, while the binaural-beat ASSR was small and only present at a 400 Hz carrier; Grose et al. found binaural-beat salience equivalent to only a shallow sinusoidal AM. Layering a monaural beat (both tones to both ears) under the binaural pair gives a stronger, speaker-compatible driving stimulus.
- **Usable parameters:** Monaural beat layer: two tones f and f + f_beat summed identically in both ears on a carrier distinct from the binaural carriers (e.g. 204.15 Hz = 1.5 x 136.1, or 340.25 Hz), at -6 to -9 dB relative to the binaural pair, same f_beat(t) schedule; equivalent to 100%-depth sinusoidal AM at f_beat.
- **Source:** Schwarz DWF, Taylor P (2005) Clin Neurophysiol 116:658-668 PMID 15721080; Grose, Buss & Hall (2012) Hear Res 285:40-45; Pratt H et al. (2010) Hear Res 262:34-44 (cortical responses to binaural vs acoustic beats)

### Isochronic tone design

- **Evidence level:** weak
- **Finding:** Isochronic tones are discrete, equal-spaced pulses of a carrier; the common generator convention is 50% duty cycle with a raised-cosine (Hann) gate so there are no onset clicks. Their specific superiority over AM/monaural beats is practitioner lore (Siever/Mind Alive) rather than demonstrated, but a Hann-gated pulse train is simply a broadband-sideband AM and does produce ASSR at the pulse rate.
- **Usable parameters:** Pulse rate = current f_beat(t); duty 50% (at 6 Hz: period 166.7 ms, on-time 83.3 ms); gate = Hann window over the on-time (rise/fall each 41.7 ms at 6 Hz); carrier distinct from binaural carriers (e.g. 204.15 or 340.25 Hz); level -6 to -12 dB relative to binaural carriers; keep identical in both channels; sidebands fall at f_c +/- k f_pulse so a 300-350 Hz carrier keeps them inside one ERB.
- **Source:** Siever D, 'The Rediscovery of Audio-Visual Entrainment Technology' (Mind Alive, 2000); Huang TL, Charyton C (2008) Altern Ther Health Med 14(5):38-50 (review of AVE/brainwave entrainment); ASSR literature (Picton et al. 2003 Int J Audiol 42:177-219)

### Shamanic drum rate

- **Evidence level:** moderate
- **Finding:** Neher demonstrated auditory driving with drum pulses at 3-8 Hz (4 Hz used for continuous driving, with 40 s stimulation / 40 s rest cycles); Maxfield found the ~4-4.5 beats/s shamanic pattern 'most inducting for theta gain' versus 3-4 beats/s; Harner/FSS journey drumming is ~4.2-5 beats/s.
- **Usable parameters:** Drum rate 4.0-4.5 Hz (240-270 BPM); start at 4.5 Hz and ease to 4.0 Hz as the beat schedule descends; keep the drum rate within +/- 0.5 Hz of the current binaural beat rate during the theta stage so the two stimuli do not fight.
- **Source:** Neher A (1961) EEG Clin Neurophysiol 13:449-451; Neher A (1962) Human Biology 34:151-160; Maxfield MC (1990) PhD dissertation, Institute of Transpersonal Psychology (ProQuest 303885457); Harner M (1980/1990) The Way of the Shaman, Foundation for Shamanic Studies

### Frame-drum synthesis model

- **Evidence level:** moderate
- **Finding:** A struck frame drum is well modelled as a few decaying circular-membrane modes (ratios 1 : 1.59 : 2.14 : 2.30 : 2.65 for (0,1),(1,1),(2,1),(0,2),(3,1)) with a fast initial pitch drop, plus a short band-limited noise burst for the skin slap, all with a few-ms Hann attack; humanised timing and velocity prevent the mechanical feel that listeners find fatiguing over 15 min.
- **Usable parameters:** f0 = 70-110 Hz (project uses 72 Hz); modes at 1.59 f0 and 2.14 f0 at -8 and -14 dB; pitch envelope +12-15% decaying with tau = 30-40 ms; amplitude tau: f0 180-300 ms, upper modes 60-120 ms; noise burst band-pass 300-3000 Hz, tau 8-15 ms, -6 dB; attack Hann 2-3 ms; body low-pass 2-pole at 1.5-2.5 kHz; timing jitter Gaussian sigma 3-6 ms, velocity +/- 8-12%, light accent every 4th beat; stereo width +/- 0.35 pan on alternating hits is acceptable (non-binaural layer).
- **Source:** Fletcher NH, Rossing TD (1998) The Physics of Musical Instruments, ch. 18 (membranes); Rossing TD (2000) Science of Percussion Instruments; standard percussion-synthesis practice (Cook PR, Real Sound Synthesis for Interactive Applications, 2002)

### Harner call-back (return) signal

- **Evidence level:** traditional (a lineage's own teaching, not a tested claim)
- **Finding:** In Foundation for Shamanic Studies core-shamanism practice the journey drumming (15-30 min, steady ~4-5 beats/s) ends with a 'call-back': four groups of seven distinct, slower beats, then a period of very rapid drumming to bring the journeyer back, then the four-times-seven again to close.
- **Usable parameters:** Call-back: 4 x 7 beats at ~1.5-2 Hz with 1.5 s gaps between groups, then 30-60 s of fast drumming at 7-8 Hz, then 4 x 7 again; precede it with a 30 s gradual slowing from 4.0 to 3.5 Hz so the change is not a shock; the project's harner_callback(fast_rate=8.0, fast_dur=30.0) already matches this.
- **Source:** Harner M, The Way of the Shaman (1980; 10th-anniversary ed. 1990), journey instructions; FSS 'Shamanic Journey Solo and Double Drumming' recordings and liner notes (shamanism.org)

### Singing-bowl mode frequencies and ratios

- **Evidence level:** **strong**
- **Finding:** Tibetan bowls vibrate in (n,0) flexural modes, n = 2..7; thin-shell theory (Terwagne & Bush eq. 5, m=0) gives f_n proportional to (n^2 - 1)/sqrt(1 + 1/n^2), i.e. ratios 1 : 2.83 : 5.42 : 8.77 : 12.87 : 17.71 relative to the (2,0) fundamental, which matches measured bowls (Inacio, Henrique & Antunes: ~1 : 2.7-2.9 : 5.2-5.5 : 8.3-8.8). Measured fundamentals of four antique bowls: 187, 236, 347, 428 Hz (radii 8.9, 7.5, 6.0, 5.9 cm). When struck, the (3,0) mode is often the strongest peak; when rubbed, the (2,0) mode dominates with lock-in harmonics at 2f, 3f.
- **Usable parameters:** Additive model: partials at f x {1, 2.83, 5.42, 8.77, 12.87, 17.7} (or use measured 1, 2.75, 5.3, 8.6, 12.4, 16.8); struck amplitudes roughly {0.6, 1.0, 0.5, 0.25, 0.12, 0.06}; rubbed amplitudes {1.0 at f, 0.1 at 2f, 0.03 at 3f, 0.3 at 2.83f}; choose fundamentals near 136.1/272.2 relations (e.g. 138.6, 207.7, 277.2 Hz) or the measured 187/236/347/428 Hz set.
- **Notes:** The dossier lists Terwagne & Bush as arXiv:1106.5657 (withdrawn); the correct identifier is 1106.6348.
- **Source:** Terwagne D, Bush JWM (2011) 'Tibetan singing bowls', Nonlinearity 24:R51-R66, arXiv:1106.6348 (PDF read this session, Table 1 and eq. 5); Inacio O, Henrique LL, Antunes J (2006) 'The dynamics of Tibetan singing bowls', Acta Acustica united with Acustica 92(4):637-653 http://projects.itn.pt/VibroImpacts/Ref_A.pdf

### Singing-bowl doublet beating, decay and strike/rim-singing types

- **Evidence level:** **strong**
- **Finding:** Each bowl mode is a near-degenerate pair split by bowl asymmetry; Terwagne & Bush observe 'two peaks separated by several Hz' producing an audible beating, with the two patterns rotated by pi/4 for (2,0) and pi/2n for higher modes. The fundamental of a good bowl rings for tens of seconds while higher modes die faster. Rubbing (rim-singing) is a stick-slip process that excites mainly (2,0) plus harmonics and makes one node follow the striker, so the listener hears an amplitude wobble tied to the striker's rotation.
- **Usable parameters:** Render each mode as a pair f(1 +/- d/2f) with split d = 1-3 Hz for (2,0) (project dossier allows 2-10 Hz), d scaling ~ n^0.5 for higher modes; T60: (2,0) 20-40 s, (3,0) 12-20 s, (4,0) 6-10 s, higher 2-5 s; strike: 3-8 ms Hann attack + 10 ms band-passed noise click; rim-singing: 3-8 s raised-cosine onset, near-sinusoidal (2,0) with 2f at -20 dB and 3f at -30 dB, AM tremolo at 2 x puja rotation rate (0.3-0.6 rev/s -> 0.6-1.2 Hz) depth 20-30%, sustain 30-60 s; one strike every 20-40 s.
- **Source:** Terwagne & Bush 2011 arXiv:1106.6348 sec. 2.2; Inacio, Henrique & Antunes 2006; physical model: Ng C, ICMC 2002 https://ccrma.stanford.edu/~carmenng/250b/icmc2002.pdf

### Overtone / throat-singing drone synthesis

- **Evidence level:** moderate
- **Finding:** Tuvan sygyt isolates single harmonics (melodies built from partials 6, 7, 8, 9, 10, 12, 13) by tuning the vocal-tract second formant (with F3 merged close to it) onto one harmonic with a very narrow bandwidth, using a raised tongue and 'ee'-like lip bell; khoomei emphasises 2-3 harmonics one to two octaves above the fundamental; kargyraa adds a sub-octave (f0/2) from the vestibular folds. Gyuto monks chant around 60-75 Hz with the 5th and 10th harmonics strongly reinforced (one-voice chord).
- **Usable parameters:** Source: band-limited pulse train or Rosenberg glottal pulse at f0 = 60-110 Hz with -12 dB/oct spectral tilt; formants as 2-pole resonators: F1 fixed 300-450 Hz (BW 80 Hz), F2 tuned to n x f0 with n stepping through {6, 8, 9, 10, 12} (BW 40-60 Hz, Q 12-20), F3 = F2 + 150 Hz (BW 60 Hz) for the sygyt whistle, +6 to +10 dB peak gain on F2/F3; step changes slewed over 150-300 ms; vibrato 4.5-5 Hz, depth 0.3-0.5%; kargyraa: add f0/2 at -6 dB; Gyuto layer: f0 = 61.7 Hz (B1) or 68.05 Hz (C#2, 136.1/2) with harmonics 5 (308.7/340.3 Hz) and 10 (617/680 Hz) boosted +10 dB, phrases 20-40 s with 3-5 s breath gaps.
- **Source:** Levin TC, Edgerton ME (1999) 'The throat singers of Tuva', Scientific American 281(3):80-87; Lindestad PA et al. (2001) J Voice 15(1):78-85; Smith H, Stevens KN, Tomlinson RS (1967) JASA 41(5):1262-1264; Wikipedia 'Tuvan throat singing' (fetched this session, partial list 6-13 confirmed)

### Tanpura / Om drone with jivari and shimmer

- **Evidence level:** moderate
- **Finding:** The tanpura's 'jivari' buzz comes from the string's grazing contact with a curved bridge (and a cotton thread that sets the contact point); the contact point creeps up the bridge over ~3-10 s as the note decays, so the harmonic emphasis moves in time (Raman 1921). Standard tuning is Pa-Sa-Sa-Sa (5-8-8-1). 136.1 Hz is Cousto's 'Earth year' tone: 2^32 / (365.2422 x 86400 s) = 136.102 Hz, a numerological not an acoustic fact.
- **Usable parameters:** Strings at 204.15 (Pa), 272.2, 272.2, 136.1 Hz; pluck cycle: one string every 2-3 s (full cycle 8-12 s); 40-60 harmonics with 1/n amplitude; jivari: multiply harmonic amplitudes by a Gaussian window on harmonic index whose centre sweeps from ~25 down to ~6 over 4-8 s after each pluck (width ~4 harmonics, +12 dB peak), plus overall low-pass decay; shimmer: 2-3 unison copies detuned +/- 0.15% (+/- 2.6 cents) giving 0.2-0.4 Hz beating at 136 Hz, with a slow random walk of +/- 0.05%; whole layer -6 to -10 dB relative to the binaural pair.
- **Source:** Raman CV (1921) 'On some Indian stringed instruments', Proc Indian Assoc Cultiv Sci 7:29-33; Valette C, Cuesta C, Castellengo M, Besnainou C (1991) 'The tampura bridge as a precursive wave generator', Acustica 74:201-208; Wikipedia 'Tanpura' (fetched this session: 5-8-8-1 tuning, 3-10 s creep confirmed); Cousto H (1978/1988) The Cosmic Octave; arithmetic computed this session

### Breath pacer encoded as a slow swell

- **Evidence level:** **strong**
- **Finding:** HRV resonance (coherent) breathing is ~0.1 Hz (6 breaths/min), refined to ~0.09 Hz (5.5 breaths/min, ~11 s per breath) on average, with individual variation but no published range; a slow amplitude/filter swell on a non-binaural layer can cue this rate. 4-7-8 breathing has a 19 s cycle (0.0526 Hz) and box breathing a 16 s cycle (0.0625 Hz). There is no evidence that an audio swell alone entrains breathing, but paced-breathing apps use exactly this cue.
- **Usable parameters:** Swell on the pink-noise bed or pad only: period 10 s (0.1 Hz) or 11 s; envelope raised-cosine with 4.5 s rise (inhale) and 5.5 s fall (exhale), depth +/- 3 dB (never more than +/- 6 dB); pair the gain swell with a low-pass sweep 2 kHz -> 6 kHz on the rise so it reads as 'breath' not 'tremolo'; 4-7-8 variant: 4 s rise, 7 s plateau at +3 dB, 8 s fall; box: 4 s rise, 4 s hold, 4 s fall, 4 s hold; never modulate the binaural carriers.
- **Source:** Lehrer PM, Gevirtz R (2014) 'Heart rate variability biofeedback: how and why does it work?' Front Psychol 5:756 https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/fpsyg.2014.00756/full (fetched this session); Steffen PR et al. (2017) Front Public Health 5:222 (5.5 bpm); cycle arithmetic computed this session

### Pink and brown noise generation

- **Evidence level:** **strong**
- **Finding:** Paul Kellet's refined 'pk3' filter gives pink noise accurate to +/- 0.05 dB above 9.2 Hz at 44.1 kHz (6 first-order sections, coefficients verified), Voss-McCartney with 16 octaves reaches down to 0.67 Hz but has ~2 dB ripple, and FFT shaping (|X(f)| x f^-1/2) is exact for a fixed block. Brown (red) noise is a leaky integration of white noise followed by a high-pass to remove DC/rumble.
- **Usable parameters:** pk3: b0=0.99886*b0+w*0.0555179; b1=0.99332*b1+w*0.0750759; b2=0.96900*b2+w*0.1538520; b3=0.86650*b3+w*0.3104856; b4=0.55000*b4+w*0.5329522; b5=-0.7616*b5-w*0.0168980; pink=b0+..+b6+w*0.5362; b6=w*0.115926 (vectorise with scipy.signal.lfilter per section); FFT method: rfft of white block, multiply by f^-0.5 (pink) or f^-1 (brown) with bins below 20 Hz zeroed, irfft, 50% overlap-add Hann between blocks; brown: y[n]=0.9995*y[n-1]+x[n], then 2nd-order Butterworth HP at 25 Hz (as in the project's brown_noise); normalise to target RMS.
- **Source:** Voss RF, Clarke J (1978) JASA 63:258-263; McCartney J / Kellet P, music-dsp list, summarised at https://www.firstpr.com.au/dsp/pink-noise/ (fetched this session)

### Noise-bed level and stereo decorrelation

- **Evidence level:** moderate
- **Finding:** The noise bed must sit well below the carriers so the binaural percept (which is only as salient as a shallow AM) is not masked; Monroe's patent specifies pink sound at least 10 dB below the beat signals. Fully independent noise per channel gives maximum width (IACC ~ 0) but injects random interaural differences in the carrier band; partially correlated noise keeps the image coherent.
- **Usable parameters:** Noise RMS -20 to -30 dB relative to the binaural carrier pair RMS (-25 dB default); decorrelation: either two independent RNG streams (seeds per channel) or mix 0.7 x shared + 0.3 x independent for IACC ~ 0.7; optionally notch the noise -6 dB in a +/- 1 ERB band around each binaural carrier (e.g. 400-470 Hz) so the carriers stay clear.
- **Source:** US 5,213,562 (>= 10 dB below); Grose, Buss & Hall 2012 (beat salience ~ shallow AM); Blauert J (1997) Spatial Hearing (IACC and perceived width)

### Infrasound and sub-40 Hz content

- **Evidence level:** **strong**
- **Finding:** Most consumer earbuds and headphones roll off below ~40-60 Hz and nothing below ~20 Hz is heard as a tone; low-frequency energy mainly eats headroom, raises intermodulation in cheap drivers and can trip loudness/true-peak limits. 'Epsilon' (< 0.5 Hz) and 'lambda' (100-200 Hz) practitioner bands are usable only as modulation rates or as mid-bass tones, not as sub-sonic audio.
- **Usable parameters:** High-pass the full mix at 25-30 Hz (4th-order Butterworth, SOS, or 2nd-order as the project already uses for brown noise); lowest intentional tone 55-68 Hz (A1 55 Hz, B1 61.7 Hz, C#2 68.05 Hz); any sub-0.5 Hz 'epsilon' element is implemented as a 0.1-0.3 Hz amplitude swell, not a tone.
- **Source:** ISO 226:2003 equal-loudness contours (threshold rise below 100 Hz); headphone measurement practice (e.g. Olive, Welti & McMullin 2013 AES 135, 'Listener preference for different headphone target curves'); dossier entry on epsilon/lambda bands

### The 110-111 Hz archaeoacoustic tone

- **Evidence level:** weak
- **Finding:** Jahn, Devereux & Ibison measured resonances of 95-120 Hz in six Neolithic chambers (Newgrange, Wayland's Smithy, Chun Quoit, Cairn Euny, Carn Euny fogou, Camster Round), most near 110 Hz; Cook, Pajot & Leuchter exposed 30 volunteers to 90-130 Hz tones in 5 Hz steps and reported that at 110 Hz left-temporal (language) activity dropped relative to right and prefrontal activity shifted. Small sample, never replicated; the Malta Hypogeum 'Oracle Room' figure (~110-111 Hz) is from informal measurement.
- **Usable parameters:** 110 Hz drone or monaural layer (not a binaural carrier: its ERB is 36 Hz and the percept would be weak) at -6 to -9 dB, optionally with a 2.5-4 s synthetic 'chamber' reverb, during the deep stage; keep it an octave-coherent sibling of 55 and 220 Hz; label as symbolic.
- **Source:** Jahn RG, Devereux P, Ibison M (1996) 'Acoustical resonances of assorted ancient structures', JASA 99(2):649-658; Cook IA, Pajot SK, Leuchter AF (2008) 'Ancient architectural acoustic resonance patterns and regional brain activity', Time and Mind 1(1):95-104 doi 10.2752/175169708X272518 (abstract page returned 403 this session; details from memory and the dossier)

### Spatialisation rates and limits

- **Evidence level:** **strong**
- **Finding:** Slow source motion is pleasant and non-distracting at rotation periods of 20-100 s; faster panning draws attention. Maximum natural ITD is ~0.66 ms (Woodworth model, head radius 8.75 cm, computed here), Haas precedence operates on 1-30 ms delays, and delays > ~1 ms between correlated tonal copies produce audible comb filtering.
- **Usable parameters:** Binaural carriers: pan 0.0, ITD 0, dry. Bowls/chant/pads: equal-power pan law (L = cos(theta), R = sin(theta), theta = (p+1) pi/4) with rotation 0.01-0.05 Hz (period 20-100 s); ITD on these layers <= 0.6 ms; Haas-width on noise/pads: 8-20 ms delay to one side at -6 dB, high-passed at 200 Hz; drum: fixed +/- 0.35 pan alternation; never sum-to-mono-check with the binaural pair (it must not be mono-compatible by design).
- **Source:** Woodworth RS (1938) Experimental Psychology (ITD = r/c (theta + sin theta)); Haas H (1951/1972) JAES 20:146-159; Blauert J (1997) Spatial Hearing; Griesinger D, 'The psychoacoustics of apparent source width' (AES); ITD computed this session

### Reverb design and placement

- **Evidence level:** **strong**
- **Finding:** Reverb convolves each ear with a long decorrelated tail, destroying the precise interaural phase relation that creates a binaural beat; it belongs only on the acoustic-instrument bus. A feedback-delay network or a synthetic exponential-noise impulse response both give a smooth, metallic-free tail for ambient use.
- **Usable parameters:** Synthetic IR: stereo exponentially decaying Gaussian noise, T60 = 3-4 s (project: 3.6 s), pre-delay 15-25 ms, time-varying low-pass so HF T60 is ~1/3 of LF T60 (damping corner ~3.8 kHz), independent L/R noise for decorrelation, early-reflection taps at 11, 17, 23, 31 ms at -6 to -12 dB; or FDN with 8 lines of mutually-prime lengths 29-97 ms, Householder/Hadamard feedback, per-line damping LP 3-5 kHz, gain set for T60 by g = 10^(-3 L/(T60 fs)); wet return -12 to -18 dB relative to dry on drum/bowl/chant bus; 0% on binaural, isochronic and monaural-beat buses.
- **Source:** Jot JM, Chaigne A (1991) 'Digital delay networks for designing artificial reverberators', AES 90th Conv.; Schroeder MR (1962) JAES 10:219-223; Moorer JA (1979) 'About this reverberation business', Computer Music J 3(2):13-28

### Click-free crossfades and layer entries

- **Evidence level:** **strong**
- **Finding:** Any gain change faster than a few ms spreads a click across the spectrum; raised-cosine (Hann half-window) fades of >= 1 s remove audible onsets, and ambient practice uses 5-10 s. Crossfading two uncorrelated signals needs an equal-power (sin/cos) law to avoid a -3 dB dip; crossfading two correlated signals (the same oscillator continuing) needs a linear (constant-gain) law. Never crossfade between two oscillators at different frequencies to change the beat; ramp the frequency instead.
- **Usable parameters:** Layer fade-in/out: raised-cosine >= 5 s (project gate_lane uses 8 s); event (bowl strike) attack 3-8 ms Hann; stage-to-stage: no amplitude crossfade on the binaural bus at all, only phase-continuous f_beat ramps; equal-power law for new uncorrelated layers: g_in = sin(pi t/2T), g_out = cos(pi t/2T).
- **Source:** Standard DSP practice; Zolzer U (2011) DAFX: Digital Audio Effects (amplitude envelopes); Roads C (1996) The Computer Music Tutorial (crossfade laws)

### Phase-continuous frequency ramps (cumulative phase)

- **Evidence level:** **strong**
- **Finding:** Instantaneous frequency must be integrated to phase (phi[n] = phi[n-1] + 2 pi f[n]/fs) so that beat-rate changes produce no discontinuity; float64 accumulation over 1.7e8 samples has negligible error (~1e-7 rad) but phase should still be wrapped modulo 2 pi at every chunk boundary to keep sin() accurate. Each ear needs its own accumulator with f_R = f_L + f_beat(t).
- **Usable parameters:** phase = (phase0 + 2*np.pi*np.cumsum(f_inst)/fs); carry phase[-1] % (2*np.pi) into the next chunk (project PhaseAcc does this); shape f_beat(t) ramps as smoothstep s = 3u^2 - 2u^3 over the ramp duration; start both ears at phase 0 with a 5 s raised-cosine fade-in so there is no initial click.
- **Source:** Smith JO, 'Mathematics of the DFT' / 'Physical Audio Signal Processing' (CCRMA) on phase accumulation; numpy float64 precision (eps = 2.2e-16); error bound computed this session

### Float64 synthesis, DC removal and dither

- **Evidence level:** **strong**
- **Finding:** Synthesise and mix in float64, remove DC/rumble with a stateful high-pass before metering, and add TPDF dither only when quantising to 16-bit (noise floor -96 dBFS); at 24-bit the quantisation floor (-144 dBFS) is below any playback chain so dither is optional but harmless. scipy.io.wavfile writes float32 or int32 WAV, not packed 24-bit; packed 24-bit requires manual byte packing, soundfile, or ffmpeg.
- **Usable parameters:** HP: 2nd-4th order Butterworth SOS at 20-25 Hz with sosfilt_zi state carried across chunks (or subtract a running mean with a 1 s exponential average); 16-bit: dither = (rng.random(n) + rng.random(n) - 1.0) LSB (TPDF, +/- 1 LSB) added before np.round, no noise shaping; 24-bit: optional TPDF at 1 LSB24 = 2^-23; write float32 WAV from scipy then `ffmpeg -i x.wav -c:a pcm_s24le` for a true 24-bit WAV; check DC: |mean| < 1e-4 per channel per stage.
- **Source:** Lipshitz SP, Wannamaker RA, Vanderkooy J (1992) 'Quantization and dither: a theoretical survey', JAES 40(5):355-375; scipy.io.wavfile documentation (supported dtypes); local check: soundfile not installed in this environment, scipy 1.18 / numpy 2.5 present

### True-peak measurement and limiting

- **Evidence level:** **strong**
- **Finding:** ITU-R BS.1770-4 true peak is measured on a 4x oversampled signal (for 48 kHz); platforms ask for <= -1 dBTP (Spotify: -1 dBTP for lossy encodes, -2 dBTP if the master is louder than -14 LUFS). A meditation master at -18 LUFS made of sines and noise has a crest factor of only 8-12 dB, so peaks land near -6 to -10 dBFS and no limiter should ever engage; a soft limiter is a safety net only.
- **Usable parameters:** True peak = 20 log10(max |resample_poly(x, 4, 1)|) per channel (project true_peak_dbtp does this chunked); target <= -1.0 dBTP (use -1.5 dBTP for safety before MP3 encoding); limiter: ceiling 0.891 (-1 dBFS) soft-knee 0.08, 5 ms look-ahead, 100-300 ms release, stereo-linked gain; log how many samples the limiter touched (target 0).
- **Source:** ITU-R BS.1770-4 (2015) Annex 2 (true-peak, 4x oversampling); Spotify loudness normalization page https://support.spotify.com/us/artists/article/loudness-normalization/ (fetched this session)

### Integrated loudness and LRA targets for meditation/sleep delivery

- **Evidence level:** **strong**
- **Finding:** Spotify normalises to -14 LUFS (Normal), -11 (Loud), -19 (Quiet); Apple Sound Check targets -16 LUFS; YouTube -14. Quiet masters receive positive gain only within their true-peak headroom (Spotify example: a -20 LUFS / -5 dBTP track is lifted only to -16 LUFS). Meditation/sleep producers typically master -20 to -16 LUFS so the track plays quietly at the listener's usual volume setting. Large loudness range (LRA) tracks get compressed by platform or loudnorm dynamic modes, so keep LRA moderate.
- **Usable parameters:** Integrated loudness -18 LUFS (acceptable -20 to -16); short-term loudness never above -12 LUFS-S; true peak <= -1.5 dBTP; LRA 6-10 LU (do not exceed 11); measure with ffmpeg `-af ebur128=peak=true -f null -` or the project's BS.1770 K-weighted integrated_lufs(); daily 12-15 min version may sit 1 dB louder (-17 LUFS).
- **Source:** Spotify loudness normalization page (fetched this session); Apple Music 'Mastered for iTunes/Apple Digital Masters' guidelines (-16 LUFS Sound Check); EBU Tech 3342 (LRA definition); producer practice (anecdotal for the -20..-16 claim)

### ffmpeg loudnorm two-pass usage

- **Evidence level:** **strong**
- **Finding:** loudnorm (verified option list from the local ffmpeg build: I/LRA/TP/measured_I/measured_LRA/measured_TP/measured_thresh/offset/linear/dual_mono/print_format) normalises to EBU R128 targets; in two-pass mode the first pass prints the measured values and the second pass applies them with linear=true so only a constant gain is applied; if the measured LRA exceeds the LRA target or the TP target cannot be met linearly, the filter silently falls back to dynamic (compressing) mode. loudnorm resamples internally to 192 kHz, so the output sample rate must be forced back to 48 kHz.
- **Usable parameters:** Pass 1: ffmpeg -i master.wav -af loudnorm=I=-18:LRA=11:TP=-1.5:print_format=json -f null - ; Pass 2: ffmpeg -i master.wav -af loudnorm=I=-18:LRA=11:TP=-1.5:measured_I=<I>:measured_LRA=<LRA>:measured_TP=<TP>:measured_thresh=<thresh>:offset=<offset>:linear=true:print_format=summary -ar 48000 -c:a pcm_s24le master_norm.wav ; confirm the summary says 'Normalization Type: Linear'. Preferred alternative: compute integrated LUFS in numpy and apply one scalar gain (no resampling, no risk of dynamic mode) and only use loudnorm as a check.
- **Source:** ffmpeg loudnorm filter documentation https://ffmpeg.org/ffmpeg-filters.html#loudnorm; `ffmpeg -h filter=loudnorm` run locally this session (defaults I=-24, LRA=7, TP=-2, linear=true)

### MP3 encoding that preserves the interaural relationship

- **Evidence level:** **strong**
- **Finding:** libmp3lame defaults to joint stereo (local build: -joint_stereo default true), which switches frame-by-frame between L/R and mid/side coding. For a binaural pair S = (L-R)/2 is as large as M, so the encoder usually picks L/R anyway, but forcing simple stereo guarantees fully independent per-channel quantisation and removes any risk of the psychoacoustic model allocating fewer bits to the side signal that carries the beat. At 320 kbps CBR the encoder's ~20 kHz low-pass is irrelevant to carriers below 1 kHz.
- **Usable parameters:** ffmpeg -i master.wav -c:a libmp3lame -b:a 320k -joint_stereo 0 -compression_level 0 -ar 48000 -id3v2_version 3 -metadata title=... out.mp3 ; LAME CLI equivalent: lame -b 320 -m s -q 0 --noreplaygain in.wav out.mp3 ; do not add -q:a (VBR) together with -b:a; expect 2.4 MB/min (project's 15-min file: 36.0 MB; 45 min = 108 MB; 60 min = 144 MB); after encoding, decode and re-run the beat-rate verification on the MP3 to prove the beat survived.
- **Source:** `ffmpeg -h encoder=libmp3lame` run locally this session; LAME USAGE documentation (-m s = simple stereo, -m j = joint stereo); ISO/IEC 11172-3 (MPEG-1 Layer III joint-stereo modes)

### FLAC delivery and file-size estimates

- **Evidence level:** **strong**
- **Finding:** 24-bit 48 kHz stereo PCM is 288 kB/s = 17.28 MB/min (60 min = 1.04 GB). Pure tonal material compresses to < 40% in FLAC, but the noise beds dominate: the project's rendered 15-min test compressed to 186.5 MB versus 259 MB of 24-bit PCM (72%), so the full track will be large.
- **Usable parameters:** ffmpeg -i master.wav -c:a flac -compression_level 8 -sample_fmt s32 out.flac (verify 24-bit with ffprobe: bits_per_raw_sample=24; if it reports 32, use the flac CLI: flac --best --bps 24 -b 4096); estimates: 15 min ~ 187 MB, 45 min ~ 560 MB, 60 min ~ 745 MB FLAC; WAV 24-bit: 259 / 778 / 1037 MB; MP3 320k: 36 / 108 / 144 MB; lowering the noise bed by 6 dB shrinks FLAC roughly 10-15%.
- **Source:** Sizes measured on /home/user/VERCEL_TOKEN/output/test_daily.flac (186,505,808 bytes for 15 min) and arithmetic computed this session; FLAC format specification (xiph.org/flac/format.html)

### Session length and stage proportions

- **Evidence level:** moderate
- **Finding:** Measured binaural effects need >= 10 min to appear and habituate by 25-30 min of a static stimulus (Jirakittayakorn 2017); Hemi-Sync Gateway exercises run ~30-45 min, Harner journeys 15-30 min of drumming plus call-back, Satyananda yoga nidra (8 stages: internalisation, sankalpa, rotation of consciousness, breath awareness, opposites, visualisation, sankalpa, externalisation) runs 20-45 min. A 48-52 min 'journey' with moving plateaus of 10-12 min fits all of these; a 12-15 min daily version keeps one 8-10 min plateau.
- **Usable parameters:** Full track 50 min: 0-5 settle (alpha 10 -> 8 Hz, tanpura + breath swell), 5-13 induction (8 -> 6 Hz, bowls enter), 13-25 theta plateau (6 -> 4.5 Hz, drum 4.5 -> 4.0 Hz), 25-35 deep (4.5 -> 3 Hz, 110 Hz chamber drone, delta+beta stack optional), 35-40 crown (40 Hz monaural AM 3-4 min over 3 Hz binaural), 40-47 return (3 -> 13 Hz at ~1.4 Hz/min, drum call-back at 40-42), 47-50 SMR close at 13 Hz and 30 s fade. Daily 14 min: 0-2 alpha 10 -> 7 Hz, 2-11 plateau 6 Hz, 11-14 return to 12-13 Hz.
- **Source:** Jirakittayakorn & Wongsawat 2017 (fetched); Monroe Institute Gateway Experience track lengths (practitioner); Harner 1980/1990; Saraswati S (1976) Yoga Nidra, Bihar School of Yoga; Wikipedia 'Yoga nidra' (fetched: 8 stages confirmed, no duration given)

### 40 Hz gamma element must be monaural/AM, and kept short

- **Evidence level:** **strong**
- **Finding:** 40 Hz is above the ~30-35 Hz binaural-beat ceiling, so a 40 Hz binaural pair produces little or no beat percept; a 40 Hz amplitude-modulated or monaural-beat tone evokes a strong auditory steady-state response (the 40 Hz ASSR is the most robust ASSR). 40 Hz AM is also fatiguing and buzzy, so it should be brief and quiet.
- **Usable parameters:** 40 Hz sinusoidal AM (depth 50-80%) on a 400-432 Hz carrier identical in both ears, 3-5 min, at -9 to -12 dB relative to the main binaural pair; fade in/out over 20 s; never run it in the sleep variant.
- **Source:** Galambos R, Makeig S, Talmachoff PJ (1981) PNAS 78:2643-2647 (40 Hz ASSR); Schwarz & Taylor 2005; Perrott & Nelson 1969; Jirakittayakorn & Wongsawat (2017) Int J Psychophysiol 120:96-107 (40 Hz binaural beat study)

### Numpy chunked rendering and memory

- **Evidence level:** **strong**
- **Finding:** A 60-min 48 kHz track is 172.8 M samples per channel: 1.38 GB per channel in float64 (2.76 GB stereo), 1.38 GB stereo in float32; the brief's figure of 345 MB per channel is wrong (345 MB is 15 min per channel at float64, i.e. exactly the size of the project's 15-min float32 stereo WAV). Rendering in 30-60 s blocks with explicit carried state keeps peak memory under ~200 MB.
- **Usable parameters:** Block = 30-60 s (1.44-2.88 M samples); per-layer state: phase accumulators (phase mod 2 pi), sosfilt zi arrays (init with sosfilt_zi(sos) * x[0]), RNG objects (np.random.default_rng(seed_per_layer)), reverb overlap carry (oaconvolve each block then add the IR-length tail into the next block), event buffers for notes longer than a block (bowl 38 s: render whole event once, add_at with carry); write each block immediately via stdlib wave (int32 or packed 24-bit) or append to a float32 file; keep a running sum of squares and max for metering so no second pass over the file is needed.
- **Source:** Arithmetic computed this session; size of /home/user/VERCEL_TOKEN/output/test_daily.wav = 345,600,058 bytes = 15 min x 48000 x 2 ch x 4 B; scipy.signal.sosfilt / sosfilt_zi / oaconvolve documentation

### Filters and resonators in scipy

- **Evidence level:** **strong**
- **Finding:** Second-order-section Butterworth filters via scipy.signal.butter(..., output='sos') + sosfilt are numerically safe at 48 kHz even for 25 Hz corners; formants and bowl modes are cheapest as 2-pole resonators whose radius sets bandwidth; long harmonic drones are cheapest as additive sums or Karplus-Strong comb loops.
- **Usable parameters:** Resonator: y[n] = G x[n] + 2 r cos(w0) y[n-1] - r^2 y[n-2], r = exp(-pi BW/fs), w0 = 2 pi f0/fs, G = (1 - r) for unity peak; formant BW 40-100 Hz; bowl modes BW 0.05-0.2 Hz (r very close to 1: use additive decaying sines instead for stability); implement as sos row [G, 0, 0, 1, -2 r cos(w0), r^2] and run with sosfilt + zi; band-pass for drum noise: butter(2, [300, 3000], 'bandpass', output='sos').
- **Source:** Smith JO, Introduction to Digital Filters (CCRMA) sec. on resonators (r = exp(-pi BW/fs)); scipy.signal documentation; Karplus K, Strong A (1983) Computer Music J 7(2):43-55

### Verification maths for beat rate per stage

- **Evidence level:** **strong**
- **Finding:** The beat rate can be measured directly from the rendered stereo file by comparing the instantaneous phases of the two ears in the carrier band, or from the envelope of the monaural sum or difference: |L - R| for equal-amplitude carriers equals 2|sin(pi f_beat t)|, whose fundamental is f_beat. A 2^20-point FFT at 48 kHz gives 0.046 Hz bins (21.8 s window), enough to separate 4.0 from 4.5 Hz; 2^18 gives 0.18 Hz.
- **Usable parameters:** Method A: band-pass L and R +/- 30 Hz around each carrier (sos), scipy.signal.hilbert on 20-60 s segments, f_beat(t) = d/dt unwrap(angle(aL) - angle(aR)) / (2 pi), median-filter 1 s, compare with the design schedule (tolerance +/- 0.05 Hz); Method B: env = |hilbert(L + R)| or |hilbert(L - R)| band-limited, Welch PSD with nperseg = 2^20, peak must be at f_beat +/- 0.05 Hz and >= 20 dB above neighbours; also per stage: integrated LUFS, true peak, DC, carrier ILD (< 0.1 dB), and after MP3 decode repeat Method A to prove the beat survived encoding.
- **Source:** Analytic-signal / Hilbert demodulation (Oppenheim & Schafer, Discrete-Time Signal Processing); FFT bin arithmetic computed this session

### Spectrogram PNG for verification

- **Evidence level:** **strong**
- **Finding:** A full-rate 60-min spectrogram is needlessly large; decimating to a few kHz and using long windows resolves the carriers, bowls and drone while keeping the image small enough to read. A separate beat-rate trace plot is more useful than trying to see 4 Hz sidebands in a spectrogram.
- **Usable parameters:** x4k = resample_poly(mono_or_L, 1, 12) (48 kHz -> 4 kHz, 0-2 kHz view); spectrogram(x4k, fs=4000, nperseg=8192, noverlap=8192-4000) (0.49 Hz resolution, 1 s hop); 10 log10(Sxx + 1e-12) clipped to [-100, -10] dB; pcolormesh with shading='auto', y-limit 0-1200 Hz for the carrier/bowl view plus a second panel 0-150 Hz for drums/chant; overlay the designed carrier lines; third panel: measured f_beat(t) vs designed schedule; save 2400x1200 px at 150 dpi.
- **Source:** scipy.signal.spectrogram / resample_poly documentation; matplotlib (3.11 present locally, use the Agg backend)

### Mix balance table (relative levels)

- **Evidence level:** anecdotal
- **Finding:** Because the binaural percept is subtle, every other layer must be mixed under or around it, not over it; practitioner mixes keep the carriers clearly audible but not dominant, with the noise bed as a floor and acoustic events as foreground accents that never exceed the carriers for long.
- **Usable parameters:** Reference: binaural carrier pair RMS = 0 dB (about -20 dBFS RMS per ear); monaural-beat / isochronic layer -6 to -9 dB; tanpura/Om drone -6 to -10 dB; chant/overtone drone -8 to -12 dB; bowls -6 dB at strike peak decaying; drum -6 to -9 dB RMS (peaks to -3 dB); 110 Hz chamber drone -6 to -9 dB; pink/brown bed -20 to -30 dB; reverb return -12 to -18 dB; breath swell +/- 3 dB on bed/pad only; overall bus gain set so integrated loudness = -18 LUFS and true peak <= -1.5 dBTP.
- **Source:** Grose, Buss & Hall 2012 (salience ~ shallow AM, i.e. easily masked); US 5,213,562 (noise >= 10 dB below); Monroe Institute / practitioner mixing conventions

### Design recommendations from this lens

- Binaural bus: three carriers at 136.1, 272.2 and 408.3 Hz (or 432 Hz for the top one), identical float amplitude in both ears, perfectly centred, no reverb, no panning, no per-channel processing; the 408/432 Hz carrier is the perceptual workhorse (0 dB), 272.2 at -3 dB, 136.1 at -6 dB.
- Pick one offset scheme and keep it for the whole track: same-offset (every right-ear carrier = left + f_beat) for a single clear beat, or ratio-scaled offsets for a 1:2:3 stacked beat; never mix the two or use opposite signs.
- Drive f_beat(t) from one smoothstep schedule shared by the binaural bus, the monaural-beat layer and the isochronic layer; ramp at <= 1 Hz/min descending and ~1.4 Hz/min ascending; hold each plateau 10-15 min and change something at each plateau to counter habituation.
- Add a monaural-beat (AM) layer on a separate carrier (204.15 or 340.25 Hz) at -6 to -9 dB and a Hann-gated 50%-duty isochronic layer at -6 to -12 dB; put the 40 Hz gamma element on this AM path for 3-5 min only, never as a binaural pair.
- Drum: physically modelled frame drum at 4.5 -> 4.0 Hz with sigma 3-6 ms jitter during the theta stage, and the Harner call-back (4x7 slow, 30-60 s at 7-8 Hz, 4x7 again) at the start of the return stage; reverb and light panning are allowed on this layer.
- Bowls: additive mode pairs using ratios 1 : 2.83 : 5.42 : 8.77 : 12.87 with 1-3 Hz doublet splits and T60 of 20-40 s on the fundamental; strike every 20-40 s; rim-singing swells of 30-60 s with 0.6-1.2 Hz tremolo; wet reverb only on this bus.
- Drone bed: tanpura 5-8-8-1 at 204.15/272.2/272.2/136.1 Hz with a sweeping jivari window and +/- 0.15% detuned copies, plus an optional 61.7 or 68.05 Hz Gyuto-style voice with 5th/10th harmonics boosted and a sygyt-style formant melody on harmonics 6-12 during the crown stage.
- Breath pacer as a +/- 3 dB raised-cosine swell with 4.5 s rise / 5.5 s fall (0.1 Hz) on the pink-noise bed and pad only, with a matching 2-6 kHz low-pass sweep; keep the noise bed at -25 dB relative to the carriers and decorrelated (independent seeds or 0.7/0.3 shared mix).
- Render in 30-60 s float64 blocks with carried phase/filter/reverb state, high-pass the mix at 25-30 Hz, scale to -18 LUFS integrated with a single scalar gain, confirm true peak <= -1.5 dBTP with 4x oversampling, and only then quantise (TPDF dither for 16-bit); deliver FLAC 24-bit (expect ~12.4 MB/min) and MP3 320 kbps with -joint_stereo 0.
- Verify every stage automatically: Hilbert phase-difference beat trace vs schedule (tolerance 0.05 Hz), envelope-PSD peak of |L - R| with a 2^20 FFT, carrier ILD < 0.1 dB, DC < 1e-4, per-stage LUFS/true peak, a 0-1200 Hz decimated spectrogram PNG, and a repeat of the beat check on the decoded MP3.

### Cautions from this lens

- The WebSearch budget for this turn was already exhausted before this lens ran, so no new web searches were possible. Verification was limited to direct fetches of primary pages (Monroe patent US 5,213,562 and US 3,884,218, Jirakittayakorn & Wongsawat 2017, Lehrer & Gevirtz 2014, the firstpr.com.au pink-noise page, Terwagne & Bush 2011 arXiv:1106.6348 PDF, Spotify loudness page, Wikipedia tanpura/throat-singing/yoga-nidra pages), local ffmpeg option listings, and the project's existing RESEARCH_DOSSIER.md. Oster 1973, Licklider 1950, Perrott & Nelson 1969, Schwarz & Taylor 2005, Neher 1961/62, Maxfield 1990, Jahn/Devereux 1996, Cook et al. 2008, Smith/Stevens/Tomlinson 1967 and the Harner call-back pattern are cited from memory and the dossier, not re-verified this turn (PubMed and Taylor & Francis pages refused the fetch). A follow-up turn can re-search these if the workflow wants fresh confirmation.
- The brief's memory figure (60 min stereo float64 at 48 kHz = 345 MB per channel) is wrong by 4x: it is 1.38 GB per channel (2.76 GB stereo). 345 MB is the 15-min float32 stereo WAV already in /home/user/VERCEL_TOKEN/output/. Chunked rendering is mandatory for the full track.
- The Monroe patent does not contain the phrase 'mind awake body asleep' or an explicit delta + beta combination; label that stacking as Monroe Institute practitioner lore, and label all patent FFR claims (including the 'pink sound enhances FFR by 30%' claim) as unvalidated commercial claims.
- Overall evidence that binaural beats entrain EEG is mixed: Ingendoh et al. 2023 (PLoS One, systematic review of 14 studies) found 5 supportive, 8 contradictory, 1 mixed. The guide must present binaural beats as a subtle percept with modest, inconsistent EEG effects; monaural/AM and drumming are the physiologically stronger drivers. None of the engineering choices here make 'pineal activation', 'inter-dimensional travel' or 'control over frequency' real; they only make the track a well-built relaxation/meditation stimulus.
- ffmpeg loudnorm silently switches to dynamic (compressing) mode when the measured LRA exceeds the target or the TP target cannot be met linearly; always read the pass-2 summary for 'Normalization Type: Linear', or apply a scalar gain computed in numpy instead. loudnorm also upsamples to 192 kHz internally, so force -ar 48000 on output.
- I could not confirm from documentation whether ffmpeg's FLAC encoder writes 24-bit samples from s32 input or pads to 32-bit; check ffprobe's bits_per_raw_sample on the output and fall back to the flac CLI with --bps 24 if needed. soundfile and pyloudnorm are not installed in this environment (numpy 2.5.3, scipy 1.18.1, matplotlib 3.11.2 and ffmpeg are).
- The dossier cites Terwagne & Bush as arXiv:1106.5657 (withdrawn); the correct paper is arXiv:1106.6348 (Nonlinearity 24:R51-R66, 2011). Inacio et al. 2006's exact per-bowl ratios were not re-read this turn; the 2.7-2.9 / 5.2-5.5 / 8.3-8.8 ranges are consistent with the thin-shell formula but should be labelled approximate.
- The 110 Hz archaeoacoustic effect rests on one 30-subject unreplicated study (Cook et al. 2008) and informal site measurements; the 136.1 Hz 'Om' tuning is a numerological derivation (Cousto), and 432 Hz has no acoustic privilege over 440 Hz. Use them as design choices, labelled as symbolic.
- Isochronic-tone superiority, specific ramp rates (<= 1 Hz/min), SMR endings and the -20 to -16 LUFS 'binaural producer' target are practitioner conventions without controlled studies; they are safe engineering defaults, not evidence-based prescriptions.
- Binaural beats require headphones with correct left/right placement; the track is intentionally not mono-compatible. Users with hearing asymmetry, tinnitus, photosensitive/audiogenic epilepsy history, or who are driving should be warned; keep 40 Hz AM brief and quiet and never in a sleep variant.

### Skeptic fact-check of this lens

*Overall reliability:* High for the DSP/engineering content (file sizes, loudness standards, ffmpeg/LAME flags, pink-noise coefficients, bowl and membrane mode ratios, ERB and ITD arithmetic all check out) and good for the core psychoacoustic citations: the Monroe patent details, Jirakittayakorn & Wongsawat 2017 time-course, Schwarz & Taylor 2005, Grose & Mamo 2012 (1/10 at 810/850 Hz), Grose/Buss/Hall 2012, Draganova 2008, Ross 2014, Lehrer & Gevirtz 2014 and the FSS drumming tempo were verified directly this turn. Main weaknesses: the '40 Hz binaural pair gives little or no response' rationale is overstated (40 Hz is the best-documented binaural-beat ASSR), the resonance-breathing 'no published range' and 'no evidence audio pacing entrains breathing' statements are wrong, the cubic-difference-tone mechanism is mislabelled, the Jahn et al. site list double-counts Carn Euny and omits Loughcrew, and the Harner call-back pattern, Perrott & Nelson detection numbers, Maxfield and Neher protocol details remain unverified (dissertation/paywalled; WebSearch budget was exhausted this turn, so verification relied on direct fetches of Europe PMC, PMC, Frontiers, Google Patents and shamanism.org). Roughly 85% of checked claims are accurate as stated; none of the errors would change the engineering design, but several would mislead the written guide if copied verbatim. Rating: moderate-to-high (B+).

| Original claim | Verdict | Corrected statement | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| 40 Hz is above the ~30-35 Hz binaural-beat ceiling, so a 40 Hz binaural pair produces little or no beat percept; the 40 Hz element must be monaural/AM. | **overstated** | Perceptually a 40 Hz binaural beat is near the detection limit, but physiologically it is the best-documented binaural-beat response: Schwarz & Taylor 2005 recorded a 40 Hz binaural-beat ASSR at a 400 Hz mean carrier; Draganova et al. 2008 (Cereb Cortex 18:1193-1200) recorded a 40 Hz ASSR to dichotic 500/540 Hz tones in MEG; Grose & Mamo 2012 found a 40 Hz binaural-beat response in 10/10 young adults at 390/430 Hz; Ross et al. 2014 (J Neurophysiol 112:1871-1884) swept binaural beats from 3-60 Hz and found the largest cortical responses at 40 Hz. The monaural/AM route is still preferable because its ASSR is larger, but 'little or no' response is wrong; say 'subtle percept, smaller but reliable ASSR'. | Europe PMC records for Draganova 2008 (PMID 17827173), Ross 2014 (doi 10.1152/jn.00224.2014), Schwarz & Taylor 2005 (doi 10.1016/j.clinph.2004.09.014); PMC3243787 (Grose & Mamo 2012) full text, fetched this session |
| Grose & Mamo found no binaural-beat response at 810/850 Hz carriers in most listeners. | **accurate** | Confirmed and can be stated more precisely: with 390/430 Hz primaries all 10 young adults showed a 40 Hz binaural-beat response; with 810/850 Hz only 1 of 10 did. Add the age finding: only 9 of 14 older adults showed the response even at the low pair, so delta/theta binaural percepts will be weaker for older listeners. | Grose JH, Mamo SK (2012) Ear Hear 33(2):187-194, PMC3243787 (full text fetched this session) |
| Detection is best for beat rates of 10-15 Hz and reaches 50% detection at ~30 Hz (Perrott & Nelson 1969); Licklider/Webster/Hedlun: largest audible beat ~35 Hz occurs at 350-500 Hz carriers. | **unverifiable** | Citation is correct (Perrott DR, Nelson MA (1969) JASA 46(6):1477-1481, doi 10.1121/1.1911890; plus Perrott's 1970 follow-up note JASA 47(2):663-664). The specific '10-15 Hz best' and '50% at 30 Hz' figures could not be confirmed from any accessible source (JASA paywalled, PubMed blocked); Grose, Buss & Hall 2012 only state that the 400-500 Hz region 'supports the widest range of beat rates' citing Licklider 1950 and Perrott & Nelson 1969. Keep the 400-500 Hz carrier recommendation; soften the numeric detection figures to 'upper limit roughly 25-35 Hz at 400-500 Hz carriers'. | Europe PMC citation records for Perrott & Nelson 1969/1970; PMC3299837 Methods 2.1.1 (fetched this session) |
| HRV resonance (coherent) breathing is ~0.1 Hz (6 breaths/min), refined to ~0.09 Hz (5.5 breaths/min) on average, with individual variation but no published range. | **overstated** | The 0.1 Hz / 0.09 Hz (5.5 bpm) values are correct (Lehrer & Gevirtz 2014 confirmed verbatim), but a published range exists: the standard resonance-frequency assessment protocol (Lehrer, Vaschillo & Vaschillo 2000, Appl Psychophysiol Biofeedback 25:177-191) tests 4.5-6.5 breaths/min (0.075-0.108 Hz, 9.2-13.3 s per breath), and Lehrer & Gevirtz 2014 add that taller people and men have lower resonance frequencies. A pacer at 10-11 s per breath sits inside that range; offering a 12-13 s option covers tall/male listeners. | Lehrer PM, Gevirtz R (2014) Front Psychol 5:756 (fetched this session); Lehrer, Vaschillo & Vaschillo 2000 (from memory) |
| There is no evidence that an audio swell alone entrains breathing, but paced-breathing apps use exactly this cue. | **overstated** | Auditory pacing does slow and entrain breathing: device-guided breathing with melodic tones (RESPeRATE) reduced respiration to <10 breaths/min and lowered blood pressure in randomized trials (Grossman E et al. 2001 J Hum Hypertens 15:263-269; Elliott WJ et al. 2004 J Clin Hypertens 6:553-559); Haas, Distenfeld & Axen (1986) J Appl Physiol 61:1185-1191 showed respiratory pattern locking to perceived musical rhythm; Bernardi, Porta & Sleight (2006) Heart 92:445-452 showed tempo-dependent respiratory changes. What is untested is a +/-3 dB noise swell specifically; the mechanism (auditory cue -> paced breathing) is established. | Grossman et al. 2001; Elliott et al. 2004; Haas et al. 1986; Bernardi et al. 2006 (from memory; WebSearch budget exhausted this turn) |
| The cubic difference tone 2f1 - f2 = 2(140.1) - 276.2 = 4.0 Hz is an acoustic (monaural) beat at exactly the beat rate, which reinforces rather than cancels. | **overstated** | The arithmetic is right but the mechanism is mislabelled. Cubic difference tones are generated for primaries within roughly f2/f1 = 1.1-1.3; here f2/f1 = 1.97 (a near-octave), and a 4 Hz 'tone' is sub-audio in any case. What actually occurs is a 4 Hz periodic change in waveform shape between a tone and its mistuned second harmonic ('beats of mistuned consonances', Plomp 1967 JASA 42:462-474), which is only faintly audible at moderate levels. The practical conclusion (same-sign offsets do not cancel; do not mix schemes) stands, but the claimed reinforcement is weak at best. | Plomp R (1967) 'Beats of mistuned consonances' JASA 42:462-474; Goldstein 1967 (CDT region); from memory |
| Jahn, Devereux & Ibison measured resonances of 95-120 Hz in six Neolithic chambers (Newgrange, Wayland's Smithy, Chun Quoit, Cairn Euny, Carn Euny fogou, Camster Round). | **misattributed** | The 95-120 Hz range and the count of six are right, but the site list double-counts one site and omits two: 'Cairn Euny' and 'Carn Euny fogou' are the same Cornish site. The structures reported were Wayland's Smithy, Chun Quoit and Carn Euny (England), Newgrange and Cairns L and I at Loughcrew/Carnbane West (Ireland), and Camster Round (Scotland). Could not be re-verified online this turn (JASA paywalled, Semantic Scholar rate-limited); flag as from memory. | Jahn RG, Devereux P, Ibison M (1996) JASA 99(2):649-658 (citation confirmed; abstract from memory) |
| Cook, Pajot & Leuchter exposed 30 volunteers to 90-130 Hz tones in 5 Hz steps. | **unverifiable** | n = 30 and the 90-130 Hz range match the published abstract as I recall it, and the 110 Hz finding (left-temporal activity lowest at 110 Hz, prefrontal asymmetry shift) is reported correctly; the '5 Hz steps' detail could not be confirmed (publisher page 403, Semantic Scholar 429). It is a single unreplicated EEG study in a non-neuroscience journal; keep evidence level 'weak'. | Cook IA, Pajot SK, Leuchter AF (2008) Time and Mind 1(1):95-104 (citation correct; details not re-verified) |
| Harner/FSS journey drumming is ~4.2-5 beats/s; call-back is four groups of seven beats, then rapid drumming, then four-times-seven again. | **unverifiable** | The Foundation for Shamanic Studies' own drumming page states the drum 'is played in a steady rhythm at approximately 5 beats per second' and that recordings 'conclude with a fast callback rhythm', so the tempo range is acceptable (note the often-quoted 205-220 bpm = 3.4-3.7 Hz figure from The Way of the Shaman is lower than the FSS recordings; label the design range 3.5-5 Hz). The specific 4x7 / fast / 4x7 call-back structure is not stated on the FSS site; a '4 sharp strokes, ~30 s rapid drumming, 4 sharp strokes' variant also circulates. Verify against the actual recording before claiming it as the FSS pattern. | https://www.shamanism.org/drumming/ (fetched this session) |
| Maxfield found the ~4-4.5 beats/s shamanic pattern 'most inducting for theta gain' versus 3-4 beats/s. | **unverifiable** | Maxfield MC (1990) PhD dissertation (Institute of Transpersonal Psychology) is real and is the usual source for the 4-4.5 bps theta claim, but it is an unpublished dissertation with a small sample and no peer-reviewed replication; could not be fetched this turn. Note the peer-reviewed counterpoint: Will & Berg 2007 (Neurosci Lett 424:55-60) found periodic drum/click stimulation at 1-8 Hz produced maximal EEG synchronization at 2 Hz, not 4-4.5 Hz. | Will U, Berg E (2007) Neurosci Lett 424(1):55-60, PMID 17709189 (Europe PMC record fetched this session) |
| Gu, Y. et al. (1995) 'Failure to hear binaural beats below threshold'. | **misattributed** | Authors are Gu X, Wright BA, Green DM (1995) JASA 97(1):701-703. The substance (beats not heard when one tone is below threshold, contradicting Groen 1964/Oster) is correct and is cited the same way in Grose, Buss & Hall 2012. | PMC3299837 Introduction (fetched this session) |
| Griesinger D, 'The psychoacoustics of apparent source width' (AES). | **misattributed** | Griesinger D (1997) 'The psychoacoustics of apparent source width, spaciousness and envelopment in performance spaces', Acta Acustica united with Acustica 83:721-731 (not an AES paper). | From memory; standard citation |
| Monroe patent US 5,213,562: up to 100 carrier pairs, 275 Hz 'most effective harmonic carrier', 100/104 Hz example, FFR at 1.5/4/6, 2/4/7, 0.5/3/4 Hz, pink sound >= 10 dB below gives up to 30% FFR enhancement, beats < 30 Hz entrain. | **accurate** | All six details confirmed verbatim in the patent text. Evidence level 'weak' (commercial patent, no peer review) is the right label. | https://patents.google.com/patent/US5213562A/en (fetched this session) |
| Jirakittayakorn & Wongsawat 2017: 250/256 Hz, 6 Hz beat, 30 min; no theta increase at 5 min, 18 of 19 positions at 10 min, 12 at 15, 4 at 20, 8 at 25, 1 at 30, none post-stimulus. | **accurate** | Confirmed exactly against Table 1 of the paper (19 analysed 10/20 positions; T4 the only non-significant site at 10 min). | Front Neurosci 11:365 (fetched this session) |
| Grose et al. found binaural-beat salience equivalent to only a shallow sinusoidal AM. | **accurate** | Confirmed; add detail: 12 listeners, rates 4/8/16/32 Hz, 500 Hz carrier region; 'matched-salience SAM tones had relatively shallow depths of modulation', all listeners perceived the beats. | Grose, Buss & Hall (2012) Hear Res 285:40-45, PMC3299837 (fetched this session) |
| Optionally notch the noise -6 dB in a +/- 1 ERB band around each binaural carrier (e.g. 400-470 Hz). | **overstated** | +/- 1 ERB around 432 Hz (ERB 71 Hz) is 361-503 Hz, not 400-470 Hz; around 408 Hz it is 340-476 Hz. | Glasberg & Moore 1990 ERB formula, arithmetic |
| Cousto H (1978/1988) The Cosmic Octave. | **accurate** | Minor date note: Cousto's 'Die kosmische Oktave' appeared in German in 1984 (calculations dated 1978), English 'The Cosmic Octave' 1988. The 136.10 Hz arithmetic (2^32 / 31,556,926 s) is correct and correctly labelled numerological. | Arithmetic; publication history from memory |
| I could not confirm whether ffmpeg's FLAC encoder writes 24-bit samples from s32 input or pads to 32-bit (cautions). | **accurate** | Resolvable: ffmpeg's native flac encoder, given s32 input, encodes 24 bits per sample (it logs 'encoding as 24 bits-per-sample' when bits_per_raw_sample is not 24); ffprobe should report bits_per_raw_sample=24. The flac CLI fallback is unnecessary but harmless. | FFmpeg libavcodec/flacenc.c behaviour (from memory); verify with ffprobe as the dossier already suggests |
| Shamanic drum rate: Neher demonstrated auditory driving with drum pulses at 3-8 Hz (4 Hz used for continuous driving, with 40 s stimulation / 40 s rest cycles). | **unverifiable** | Neher A (1961) EEG Clin Neurophysiol 13:449-451 (PMID 14478806) and Neher (1962) Human Biology 34:151-160 are correctly cited and used drum stimulation at rates of about 3-8 beats/s (3, 4, 6, 8 per most secondary accounts). The 40 s / 40 s protocol detail could not be confirmed. Note the standard critique: Rouget G (1985) Music and Trance (Univ. Chicago Press) argues Neher's 'driving' explanation does not account for real ceremonial drumming (tempos, intensities and durations differ), and Neher's n was small with no controls. | Europe PMC record for Neher 1961; Rouget 1985 from memory |

*Items the lens missed, added by the skeptic:*

- Perrott DR, Musicant AD (1977) 'Rotating tones and binaural beats' JASA 61:1288-1292, and Ross et al. 2014: below roughly 2-3 Hz a binaural beat is not heard as loudness fluctuation but as a single sound image moving/rotating between the ears. The design's 0.5-3 Hz 'deep/delta' binaural stages will be perceived as slow lateral motion, which conflicts with the rule 'binaural carriers: pan 0, no motion' and should be anticipated in the mix and listening guide.
- Equal-loudness (ISO 226) correction for the carrier stack: at the quiet playback levels intended (-18 LUFS master, ~50-60 phon), a 136.1 Hz tone needs roughly 8-12 dB more SPL than a 432 Hz tone to sound equally loud, so 'carriers within 6 dB of each other' in float amplitude will make the 136 Hz pair nearly inaudible on many headphones; specify levels in phon or test on a headphone target curve.
- Sensation-level dependence of the binaural percept: Licklider et al. 1950 and Tobias (1963 JASA 35:1442-1447; 1965) report that beats are clearest at low-to-moderate sensation levels (~20-50 dB SL) and that above ~1 kHz they require an alert, 'properly set' listener; a recommended headphone level (and the instruction to set volume by the carrier, not the drum) belongs in the guide.
- Age effect on binaural beats: Grose & Mamo 2012 found a 40 Hz binaural-beat response in only 9 of 14 older adults vs 10 of 10 young adults (temporal-fine-structure decline); the guide should note that older listeners may need lower carriers (250-400 Hz) and will get more from the monaural/AM and drum layers.
- Karino S et al. (2006) 'Neuromagnetic responses to binaural beat in human cerebral cortex' J Neurophysiol 96:1927-1938: MEG responses to 4.0 and 6.66 Hz binaural beats at 240 and 480 Hz carriers, the key paper for theta-rate beats in the 240-480 Hz region the design actually uses.
- Orozco Perez HD, Dumas G, Lehmann A (2020) 'Binaural beats through the auditory pathway: vestibular, neural and cortical levels' eNeuro 7(2): monaural beats produced stronger cortical entrainment than binaural beats and neither changed mood/arousal; directly supports the dossier's monaural-layer recommendation and should be cited.
- Garcia-Argibay M, Santed MA, Reales JM (2019) 'Efficacy of binaural auditory beats in cognition, anxiety, and pain perception: a meta-analysis' Psychol Res 83:357-372 (22 studies, small-to-medium effect, larger with longer exposure) and Chaieb L et al. (2015) Front Psychiatry 6:70 review: the two standard summaries a reviewer will expect alongside Ingendoh 2023.
- Null and negative EEG studies that should be named explicitly: Vernon D et al. (2014) Int J Psychophysiol 93:134-139 (alpha/beta beats, no EEG change); Lopez-Caballero F, Escera C (2017) Front Hum Neurosci 11:557 (no EEG power or arousal change); Goodin P et al. (2012) PLoS One 7:e34789 (high-density EEG, no 7 Hz steady-state entrainment); Wahbeh H et al. (2007) J Altern Complement Med 13:25-32 (7 Hz beat single session: no EEG change, small anxiety reduction).
- Kuwada S, Yin TCT, Wickesberg RE (1979) 'Response of cat inferior colliculus neurons to binaural beat stimuli' Science 206:586-588, and Wernick & Starr 1968 (superior olive): the neurophysiological basis for the 'interaural phase comparison / phase-locking limit' claim, which is asserted without a source.
- Will & Berg 2007 (Neurosci Lett 424:55-60): periodic drum sounds and clicks at 1-8 Hz entrain EEG with maximal synchronization at 2 Hz, plus Gingras B et al. (2014) PLoS One 9:e102103 'Defining the shamanic state of consciousness' (Harner-style drumming, absorption, HRV); these are the peer-reviewed drumming papers a reviewer expects, with Rouget 1985 as the ethnomusicological critique of Neher.
- 40 Hz human sensory-stimulation trials (GENUS): Chan D et al. (2022) PLoS One 17:e0278412; He Q et al. (2021) Alzheimers Dement (N Y) 7:e12178; Cimenser A et al. (2021) Front Syst Neurosci 15:746859; and the mouse work Martorell AJ et al. (2019) Cell 177:256-271. They used 40 Hz click/tone trains at roughly 60 dB SPL for 1 h/day, which bears on the dossier's 3-5 min, -9 to -12 dB 40 Hz AM element (too short/quiet to mirror those protocols, and the dementia claims should not be imported).
- ITU-R BS.1770-4 integrated-loudness gating details needed for the project's hand-rolled integrated_lufs(): absolute gate -70 LUFS, relative gate -10 LU, 400 ms blocks with 75% overlap, K-weighting (high-shelf + RLB high-pass) coefficients re-derived for 48 kHz; EBU Tech 3342 LRA uses 3 s short-term windows, -20 LU relative gate and the 10th-95th percentile. Quiet meditation material with long low-level passages is exactly where gating errors change the reading by several LU.
- Tuvan/Mongolian overtone acoustics primary papers: Adachi S, Yamada M (1999) 'An acoustical study of sound production in biphonic singing, Xöömij' JASA 105:2920-2932 (F2/F3 clustering model that the dossier's synthesis recipe is based on) and Bloothooft G et al. (1992) 'Acoustics and perception of overtone singing' JASA 92:1827-1836; the lens cites Wikipedia for the partial list instead.
- Provenance note for the 'Tibetan' singing bowl layer: there is no documented use of singing bowls in Tibetan Buddhist ritual; the 'Tibetan singing bowl' is a 20th-century Western/Nepalese market category (see e.g. Ben Joffe 2015 'Tibet's Singing Bowl Myth'; Robert Beer; Tsering Shakya). The physics citations are fine, but the cultural label should be flagged as modern.
- Solfeggio tones (963/852 Hz etc.) are mentioned only in passing; if they are implemented as drone layers the guide must state they were invented by Joseph Puleo and Leonard Horowitz in 'Healing Codes for the Biological Apocalypse' (1999) and have no connection to Guido d'Arezzo or Gregorian chant; likewise 432 Hz has no historical or acoustic privilege (Verdi's 1884 proposal was 432 Hz for practical reasons; the 'ancient tuning' story is modern).
- Resonance-frequency breathing range 4.5-6.5 breaths/min and the device-guided-breathing literature (RESPeRATE: Grossman 2001; Elliott 2004) as the evidence base for the audio breath pacer; also Zaccaro A et al. (2018) Front Hum Neurosci 12:353 review of slow-breathing effects on EEG/HRV.
- Hearing-safety and listening-level guidance: WHO/ITU safe-listening limits (80 dBA for 40 h/week) and the fact that a -18 LUFS master played at typical phone volume is well below that; plus an explicit statement that no case of audiogenic seizure from binaural beats is documented, so the epilepsy caution is precautionary (fast isochronic/flicker-like pulses at 15-25 Hz are the only element with any theoretical photosensitive-type analogue).

## 6. Safety, contraindications, protocol and honest expectations

*Lens scope:* SAFETY, CONTRAINDICATIONS, LISTENING PROTOCOL AND HONEST EXPECTATIONS

### Seizure risk (audio-only entrainment)

- **Evidence level:** moderate
- **Finding:** There are no published case reports of binaural beats or audio-only entrainment provoking seizures; the well-documented reflex trigger is flickering LIGHT, not sound. Photosensitive epilepsy affects about 3% of people with epilepsy; flicker at 16-25 Hz is most provocative, the usual trigger range is 3-30 Hz, and sound/music is not listed as a trigger. (Musicogenic epilepsy exists but is a very rare reflex epilepsy triggered by specific songs, unrelated to beat frequency.) Producer warnings about seizures are precautionary, not evidence-based.
- **Usable parameters:** No audio beat band needs to be avoided on seizure grounds. DO NOT bundle strobing/flashing visuals (3-30 Hz, esp. 16-25 Hz) with the track and say so in the guide. Keep the standard line: 'If you have a tendency toward seizures, consult your physician before use' (Hemi-Sync wording).
- **Notes:** Hemi-Sync explicitly says not to combine its audio with other brain-influencing devices (AVE light glasses, CES, tDCS). The seizure line in every producer disclaimer is inherited from audio-visual entrainment devices, where light flicker is a genuine trigger.
- **Source:** Epilepsy Society UK, Photosensitive epilepsy: https://epilepsysociety.org.uk/about-epilepsy/epileptic-seizures/seizure-triggers/photosensitive-epilepsy ; absence of case reports in the binaural-beat literature reviewed in Garcia-Argibay et al. 2019 (PMID 30073406)

### Producer warning text: Hemi-Sync / Monroe (verified)

- **Evidence level:** **strong**
- **Finding:** Hemi-Sync's official Listening Tips carry these cautions verbatim: 'Do not listen while driving or operating heavy equipment'; 'DO NOT listen to Hemi-Sync without first consulting your physician' if you have 'a tendency toward seizures, auditory disorders, or adverse mental conditions'; 'In the unlikely event that you experience any unusual physical or mental discomfort, immediately discontinue use'; products 'are not intended to replace medical diagnosis and treatment'; and 'We do not recommend using Hemi-Sync in combination with other devices or technologies that influence brain activity.' Falling asleep is explicitly fine; children may use all nonverbal recordings; most recordings run 35-60 min.
- **Usable parameters:** Copy the four-part structure into the guide: (1) no driving/machinery, (2) physician consult for seizures/auditory disorders/mental conditions, (3) discontinue on discomfort, (4) not a medical substitute. Headphones 'that enclose the ear are preferred'; 'Adjust the volume to a comfortable level'; if feeling spacey afterwards 'count down slowly from 10 to 1 or splash cool water on your face'.
- **Notes:** monroeinstitute.org returned HTTP 429 throughout this session, so the Monroe Institute's own FAQ page could not be read; hemi-sync.com (the Monroe product arm) was used instead. The task's claim that Hemi-Sync caps use at 2-3 sessions/day could NOT be verified on any fetched page; the FAQ only says training products 'work best when you listen multiple times'.
- **Source:** Hemi-Sync Help Center, Listening Tips: https://hemi-sync.com/help-center/category/listening-tips ; Hemi-Sync FAQs: https://hemi-sync.com/help-center/category/hemi-sync-faqs/

### Producer warning text: iAwake Technologies (verified)

- **Evidence level:** **strong**
- **Finding:** iAwake's disclaimer is the most explicit in the industry: people with epilepsy 'should not use'; anyone prone to seizures, pregnant, wearing a pacemaker, with a history of severe mental illness, photosensitivity, tinnitus, or taking medication/drugs should consult a physician first; children under 18 should be examined by a physician for epilepsy/seizure-prone conditions before use; 'DO NOT LISTEN TO ANY iAWAKE AUDIO FILES WHILE DRIVING OR OPERATING MACHINERY'; not to be used 'while under the influence of alcohol or other mood altering substances'; 'not intended as a replacement for medical or psychological treatment'; no warranty of 'accuracy, likely results, or reliability'; the user assumes all risk.
- **Usable parameters:** Use iAwake's list as the contraindication checklist: epilepsy (do not use), seizure-prone, pregnancy, pacemaker, severe mental illness, photosensitivity, tinnitus, medications/drugs, under-18, alcohol/drugs, driving. iAwake volume guidance: 'highest COMFORTABLE volume, with your eyes closed and in a sitting position'. Beginner schedule: build up to '1 hour daily, preferably in the morning', cycle 3 days primary tracks + 1 day 'releasing' tracks; advance tiers only when tracks stop feeling strong.
- **Notes:** iAwake's pregnancy/pacemaker/tinnitus FAQ sub-pages returned 404, so their stated rationale could not be read; the pattern is precautionary rather than evidence-based.
- **Source:** iAwake Disclaimer: https://www.iawaketechnologies.com/disclaimer/ ; FAQ 'Is there anyone who should not listen': https://www.iawaketechnologies.com/faq/is-there-anyone-who-should-not-listen-to-profound-meditation-or-other-iawake-audio-tracks/

### Producer warning text: Brain.fm and Holosync/Centerpointe (verified)

- **Evidence level:** **strong**
- **Finding:** Brain.fm's Terms contain NO medical, seizure, driving or 'not a medical device' language; they only disclaim warranties 'about the Products or Product Content, including ... efficacy' and carve out liability 'for instances of any physical harm, gross negligence and willful misconduct'. Centerpointe/Holosync publishes no medical disclaimer at all on its main or FAQ pages, states 'Holosync, though quite powerful, is totally safe when used properly', requires 'stereo headphones for ANY Centerpointe soundtrack', suggests about 30 minutes a day or 'Wake up one hour before you want to get up', and markets 'Meditate 8x faster than a Zen monk' and 'years of meditation practice in about 30 minutes a day' with no cited evidence.
- **Usable parameters:** Do not copy Holosync-style claims ('8x faster', 'years of meditation in 30 min'). Do copy the Brain.fm-style efficacy disclaimer ('no representation as to efficacy') plus the Hemi-Sync/iAwake medical cautions, which Brain.fm omits.
- **Notes:** The 'totally safe' + 'changes begin the first time you listen' framing is marketing; Holosync's claim that it 'isn't a 30-day miracle' is the only tempering statement found.
- **Source:** Brain.fm Terms: https://www.brain.fm/terms ; Centerpointe home: https://www.centerpointe.com/ ; Centerpointe FAQ: https://www.centerpointe.com/faq/

### Pacemakers / implanted cardiac devices (honest statement)

- **Evidence level:** moderate
- **Finding:** Headphone audio delivers no electrical or electromagnetic stimulation to the body, so binaural beats cannot interfere with a pacemaker or ICD in the way CES/tDCS/PEMF/AVE devices can. The only real hazard is the neodymium MAGNET in headphone drivers: a Heart Rhythm study found clinically significant magnetic interference when portable headphones were placed directly over implanted devices (on the chest), and the Heart Rhythm Society/AHA advise keeping headphones at least 15 cm (6 inches) from the device. Producer 'consult your physician if you have a pacemaker' lines are boilerplate inherited from electrical-stimulation products.
- **Usable parameters:** Guide wording: 'Binaural audio has no electrical output and does not affect pacemakers. If you have an implanted device, simply keep headphones on your head and never rest them on your chest (keep >15 cm from the device).'
- **Notes:** Honest answer to the brief: pacemaker warnings are irrelevant to audio content itself; the magnet-proximity point is the only legitimate residual.
- **Source:** Lee S. et al., 'Clinically significant magnetic interference of implanted cardiac devices by portable headphones', Heart Rhythm 2009;6(10):1432-1436 (from prior knowledge; publisher page not fetched this session); iAwake disclaimer (pacemaker consult line) https://www.iawaketechnologies.com/disclaimer/

### Pregnancy

- **Evidence level:** weak
- **Finding:** No study has examined binaural beats in pregnancy and no mechanism exists for harm at safe volumes; the iAwake 'consult a physician' line is precautionary. The genuine pregnancy-specific considerations are postural (prolonged flat supine lying after ~20 weeks can cause supine hypotension; side-lying or semi-reclined is standard advice) and volume (same WHO limits as anyone).
- **Usable parameters:** Guide wording: 'There is no evidence that this audio harms pregnancy. If pregnant, listen semi-reclined or on your left side rather than flat on your back, keep volume at or below 60%, and check with your midwife/obstetrician if you have any concerns.'
- **Notes:** Be honest that the warning is a legal precaution; do not imply a known risk.
- **Source:** iAwake disclaimer (precautionary language): https://www.iawaketechnologies.com/disclaimer/ ; general obstetric advice on supine positioning (prior knowledge, not fetched this session); WHO safe listening Q&A https://www.who.int/news-room/questions-and-answers/item/deafness-and-hearing-loss-safe-listening

### Psychiatric risk: documented adverse effects of deep contemplative/trance states

- **Evidence level:** **strong**
- **Finding:** Adverse events from meditation-type practices are real and measurable: a 2020 systematic review of 83 studies (6,703 participants) found an overall adverse-event prevalence of 8.3% (3.7% in experimental studies, 33.2% in observational), most commonly anxiety (33%), depression (27%) and cognitive anomalies (25%), occurring also in people with no prior mental-health history. In a purposive sample of 60 Western Buddhist practitioners with difficult experiences, 82% reported fear/anxiety/panic/paranoia, 43% re-experienced traumatic memories, 73% had moderate-to-severe impairment, 17% reported suicidality and 17% were hospitalised; median symptom duration was 1-3 years; 41% were practising 10+ h/day and 72% had onset during or just after a retreat (i.e., intensity/dose is the key correlate).
- **Usable parameters:** Dose-limit the protocol: one 45-60 min session/day (or two 15-20 min), hard cap 90 min/day, no multi-hour marathons, no stacking with retreats/fasting/sleep deprivation. Screen out (or require clinician sign-off for): current psychosis or bipolar mania, dissociative disorders, PTSD with active flashbacks, recent psychiatric hospitalisation, active suicidality. List Cheetah House as the go-to resource for meditation-related difficulties.
- **Notes:** The Lindahl sample was deliberately selected for difficult experiences and is not a prevalence estimate; Farias gives the prevalence. Neither studied binaural audio directly; extrapolation is to deep theta/delta trance with OBE/dissociative intent, which is closer to intensive practice than to casual relaxation.
- **Source:** Farias M. et al., Acta Psychiatrica Scandinavica 2020;142(5):374-393, doi:10.1111/acps.13225 ; Lindahl J.R. et al., 'The varieties of contemplative experience', PLOS ONE 2017, https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371/journal.pone.0176239 ; Cheetah House (support nonprofit, Brown University-affiliated researchers): https://www.cheetahhouse.org/

### Dissociation, derealisation and 'leaving the body' intent

- **Evidence level:** moderate
- **Finding:** Deliberately cultivating out-of-body or 'between dimensions' states is phenomenologically a dissociative exercise; standard lucid-dreaming/sleep-onset practice guidance already warns that 'some people experience dissociative states, and those with certain mental illnesses may be especially vulnerable', that failed attempts can cause nightmares and false awakenings, and recommends discussing plans with a doctor or mental-health professional first. People with depersonalisation/derealisation disorder, dissociative disorders, psychosis-spectrum conditions or trauma-related dissociation should not use a track whose stated aim is to loosen body-boundary.
- **Usable parameters:** Guide stop-rule: if a feeling of unreality, detachment from the body, or 'not being sure what is real' persists more than a few hours after a session, stop using the track and talk to a clinician. Build a mandatory 'return and re-embody' segment (3-5 min) at the end of every session (see grounding finding).
- **Notes:** Framing matters: present the end-of-session return as part of the practice (as Monroe and Harner do), not an optional extra.
- **Source:** Sleep Foundation, Lucid Dreams (cautions section): https://www.sleepfoundation.org/dreams/lucid-dreams ; Lindahl 2017 (sense-of-self and perceptual domains) https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371/journal.pone.0176239

### Hearing-safe levels: NIOSH / WHO figures

- **Evidence level:** **strong**
- **Finding:** NIOSH recommended exposure limit: 85 dBA averaged over 8 h, 3-dB exchange rate (every +3 dB halves safe time). WHO weekly 'sound allowance': 80 dB for 40 h/week; 85 dB 12 h 30 min; 90 dB 4 h; 95 dB 1 h 15 min; 100 dB 20 min; 60 dB and below unlimited. WHO recommends device volume 'no more than 60% of maximum', staying 'below 80 dB average' if using a level-monitoring app, 'well-fitted, and noise cancelling headphones' so you do not raise volume to beat ambient noise, resting ears 10 min per hour in loud settings, and keeping children's volume below 60%. Over 1 billion people aged 12-35 are at risk of hearing loss from recreational sound.
- **Usable parameters:** Target playback 55-70 dBA ('quiet conversation'); never above 75 dBA for a 60-min session (a 60-min session at 75 dBA uses <5% of the WHO weekly allowance). Instruct: set volume to 50-60% of device max, lower than normal music. Master the file with ample headroom (e.g., -18 to -16 LUFS integrated, true peak <= -1 dBTP) so that 60% device volume is comfortable and users are not tempted to boost. Low-frequency carriers (100-250 Hz) sound quieter than they measure, so add a short 1 kHz calibration tone at -20 dBFS at the start for setting level.
- **Notes:** The popular '60/60 rule' (60% volume for max 60 minutes) is an audiologists' rule of thumb; WHO's pages state the 60% part but not the 60-minute part. The time-based limits above are the authoritative version.
- **Source:** NIOSH Noise and Occupational Hearing Loss: https://www.cdc.gov/niosh/noise/about/noise.html ; WHO Safe Listening Q&A: https://www.who.int/news-room/questions-and-answers/item/deafness-and-hearing-loss-safe-listening ; WHO Make Listening Safe: https://www.who.int/activities/making-listening-safe

### Headphone type and the physics of binaural delivery

- **Evidence level:** **strong**
- **Finding:** A binaural beat exists only when each ear receives its own carrier in isolation (Oster 1973: perceived for carriers below ~1000 Hz and frequency differences below ~30 Hz, strongest around 400-500 Hz carriers). Interaural separation therefore determines whether the effect is binaural at all: insert earphones give ~55-70 dB interaural attenuation, supra-aural/over-ear ~40 dB, loudspeakers near 0 dB (the beat becomes an ordinary acoustic/monaural beat), and bone-conduction headsets ~0 dB because bone conducts sound to both cochleae. Hemi-Sync accepts speakers for its products but prefers headphones 'that enclose the ear'; Holosync requires stereo headphones.
- **Usable parameters:** Guide: use wired or Bluetooth STEREO headphones; closed-back over-ear or in-ear best; open-back acceptable in a silent room; flat 'sleep headband' earphones fine for lying down; NOT bone-conduction, NOT speakers, NOT mono/'spatial audio'/crossfeed/'3D' DSP modes, no EQ, no 'hearing aid transparency'. Confirm L/R orientation. Bluetooth is fine (no latency issue for a pre-mixed file). Carriers 100-400 Hz, beat <= 30 Hz as per Oster.
- **Notes:** Some manufacturers' 'spatial audio' or 'adaptive EQ' features process left/right independently and can collapse the interaural difference; tell users to switch them off.
- **Source:** Oster G., 'Auditory Beats in the Brain', Scientific American 1973;229(4):94-102, doi:10.1038/scientificamerican1073-94 ; interaural-attenuation values are standard clinical audiology (prior knowledge) ; Hemi-Sync Listening Tips https://hemi-sync.com/help-center/category/listening-tips ; Centerpointe FAQ https://www.centerpointe.com/faq/

### Driving, machinery, water and other 'situational' hazards

- **Evidence level:** **strong**
- **Finding:** Every reputable producer prohibits use while driving or operating machinery (Hemi-Sync: 'Do not listen while driving or operating heavy equipment'; iAwake: 'DO NOT LISTEN ... WHILE DRIVING OR OPERATING MACHINERY').
